			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Tokenomics at scale: Track and cap Amazon Bedrock spend per IAM principal with CUR 2.0 and AWS Budgets</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/tokenomics-at-scale-track-and-cap-amazon-bedrock-spend-per-iam-principal-with-cur-2-0-and-aws-budgets/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Arun Chandapillai]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 19:29:55 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[AWS Budgets]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">e0c369a0dd940a1e529c2070289fe5f5a34b16a2</guid>

					<description>Get per-identity Amazon Bedrock cost visibility and enforceable spend limits using only AWS-native features, with no application code for the common case. Attribute spend with IAM principal tags, report with CUR 2.0 or Cost Explorer, and enforce with AWS Budgets actions that apply a Deny policy when a threshold is crossed.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Generative AI spend is hard to attribute. Before an organization expands AI access, CFOs and CTOs usually want three answers: what do we spend per person or per team, can we cap it, and how much work does it take to get there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In our previous post, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/tokenomics-at-scale-how-jamf-built-real-time-spend-enforcement-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Tokenomics at Scale: How Jamf Built Real-Time Spend Enforcement for Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, we explained a custom enforcement loop built on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/identity-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS IAM Identity Center&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt; over &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;model invocation logs&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/lambda/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt;. This approach gives near-real-time control, but it requires code you then own and operate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post shows how to get per-identity Amazon Bedrock cost visibility and enforceable spend limits using only AWS-native features, with no application code to write for the common case. The approach relies on three things: &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-iam-principal-tracking.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM principal attribution&lt;/a&gt;, which Amazon Bedrock records automatically; &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cur/latest/userguide/table-dictionary-cur2.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost and Usage Reports&lt;/a&gt; (CUR) 2.0 or &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-cost-explorer/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Cost Explorer&lt;/a&gt; for reporting; and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-budgets/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Budgets&lt;/a&gt; with budget actions for enforcement. By the end, you will have per-identity cost reporting and an automatic guardrail that restricts Amazon Bedrock access when a threshold is crossed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The solution has three parts: attribute, report, and enforce.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Attribute.&lt;/strong&gt; Amazon Bedrock captures the AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) principal identity, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_users.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM user&lt;/a&gt; or &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;role&lt;/a&gt;, for every inference request. This happens automatically, with no change to your Amazon Bedrock model inference calls. If several people share one role, you add per-user detail with session tags passed at role assumption time. If each person or application has its own role, principal tags on the role are enough.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Report.&lt;/strong&gt; Activate those tags as cost allocation tags, then read the data either in Cost Explorer for a console-only view, or in a CUR 2.0 data export for detailed line-item analysis and SQL queries.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Enforce.&lt;/strong&gt; Create an AWS Budget scoped to Amazon Bedrock and filtered to one identity or tag value, then attach a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cost-management/latest/userguide/budgets-controls.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;budget action&lt;/a&gt; that applies a Deny IAM policy when the threshold is crossed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following sections show each part in more detail, alongside the implementation steps.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To deploy this solution, you need:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/account/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt; with Amazon Bedrock model access enabled.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Permissions to tag &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-iam-principal-tracking.html#cost-mgmt-iam-principal-tracking-principal-types" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;IAM principal&lt;/a&gt;, activate &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/awsaccountbilling/latest/aboutv2/cost-alloc-tags.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cost allocation tags&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cur/latest/userguide/dataexports-create.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;create data exports&lt;/a&gt;, and create budgets and budget actions.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock accessed through IAM roles or IAM users.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/cli/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Command Line Interface&lt;/a&gt; (AWS CLI) configured with appropriate credentials.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A CUR 2.0 data export and an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service&lt;/a&gt; (&lt;em&gt;Amazon S3&lt;/em&gt;) bucket to receive it. &lt;em&gt;(optional, for detailed line-item analysis)&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Athena. &lt;em&gt;(optional, for querying the export)&lt;/em&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/aws-billing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Billing Console&lt;/a&gt; access is managed separately from most IAM permissions. Confirm you have it before starting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="walkthrough"&gt;Walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before configuring anything, check what you already have. Amazon Bedrock records caller identity for every inference request automatically. Open Cost Explorer, filter Service to Amazon Bedrock, and group by the available identity or tag dimension. For accounts where each application or person already uses a distinct IAM role, this may be all the visibility you need.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To configure additional visibility:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Step 1 gives you identity-level visibility.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Steps 2 through 4 add tag dimensions and detailed reporting.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Step 5 adds enforcement.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-decide-how-identity-maps-to-your-users"&gt;Step 1: Decide how identity maps to your users&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;How you tag depends on how your IAM principals are organized.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;One role per person or per application.&lt;/strong&gt; Attach principal tags directly to each IAM role or user. These are static and apply to every request from that principal. Use the IAM console, or the AWS CLI:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-bash"&gt;aws iam tag-role \
  --role-name BedrockDeveloperRole-alice \
  --tags Key=user,Value=alice@example.com Key=team,Value=platform Key=CostCenter,Value=CC-1001&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A shared role used by several people, or a gateway.&lt;/strong&gt; Principal tags cannot distinguish users on a shared role, because every session carries the same tags. Use session tags instead, passed when the role is assumed. Session tags are dynamic and can vary per session. If a session tag and a principal tag share a key, the session tag wins for that session.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The role’s trust policy must allow &lt;code&gt;sts:TagSession&lt;/code&gt;. Session tags can be passed three ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;sts:AssumeRole&lt;/code&gt; with &lt;code&gt;--tags&lt;/code&gt;, for a gateway assuming a role per user or tenant.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity&lt;/code&gt;, with tags in the https://aws.amazon.com/tags claim of the OpenID Connect (OIDC) ID token (this is a claim name, not a URL you browse to).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;AssumeRoleWithSAML&lt;/code&gt;, by mapping &lt;code&gt;PrincipalTag:*&lt;/code&gt; attributes in the identity provider’s Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) assertion.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your organization already captures a claim like &lt;code&gt;saml:sub&lt;/code&gt; for other purposes, that alone does not make it available here. Your identity provider must separately map that attribute to a &lt;code&gt;PrincipalTag:*&lt;/code&gt; SAML attribute statement, and the role’s trust policy must allow &lt;code&gt;sts:TagSession&lt;/code&gt;, before it flows through as a session tag. The two integrations draw from the same SAML assertion but are configured independently. Common tag keys are user, team, Department, CostCenter, and Environment. You can apply up to 50 principal or session tags per IAM user or role.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-1.png" alt="Principal tags attached to an IAM role compared with session tags passed at role assumption" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Attribution with principal tags compared to session tags&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-activate-cost-allocation-tags"&gt;Step 2: Activate cost allocation tags&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Tags do not appear in billing data until you activate them.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Open the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aws-cost-management/billing-and-cost-management-console-home/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Billing and Cost Management Console&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Cost allocation tags&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Filter by type &lt;strong&gt;IAM principal&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Select your tag keys and choose &lt;strong&gt;Activate&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In an &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/organizations/latest/userguide/orgs_introduction.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Organization&lt;/a&gt;, activate IAM principal cost allocation tags in the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/managedservices/latest/userguide/management-account.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;management account&lt;/a&gt; (payer). Member accounts cannot activate them independently.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two things to expect:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A tag key only appears in this list after the principal has made at least one Amazon Bedrock call, so make a test call first if the list looks empty.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;It might take up to 24 hours for a newly applied tag key to appear for activation, then up to another 24 hours after you activate it before it begins populating billing data.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Activation does not apply retroactively by default, so activate tags before you need the data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-create-a-cur-2.0-data-export-that-includes-caller-identity"&gt;Step 3: Create a CUR 2.0 data export that includes caller identity&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Skip this step if Cost Explorer is enough for your reporting.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In the Billing and Cost Management console, choose &lt;strong&gt;Data Exports&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose &lt;strong&gt;Create&lt;/strong&gt; and create a CUR 2.0 (Standard) export.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Additional export content&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;Include caller identity (IAM principal) allocation data&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Time granularity&lt;/strong&gt;, select Hourly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Report data integration&lt;/strong&gt;, select &lt;strong&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/strong&gt;. This sets the file format to Parquet and, importantly, delivers a setup script to your S3 bucket alongside the export data.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Choose the destination Amazon S3 bucket.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run the delivered setup script (or the Glue crawler/DDL it defines) once. This is what actually creates the Glue Data Catalog table you’ll query in Step 4. The preceding console flow does not create it for you automatically.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you already have a CUR 2.0 export created before enabling identity attribution, it will not include identity data retroactively. Create a new export with the caller identity option selected. In the export, principal and session tags appear as columns prefixed &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/&lt;/code&gt;, and resource tags as &lt;code&gt;resourceTags/&lt;/code&gt;. For example, a tag key team appears as &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/team&lt;/code&gt;. AWS publishes a ready-made query set for this exact pattern in &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-cur-iam-principal-bedrock-tracking" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;aws-samples/sample-cur-iam-principal-bedrock-tracking&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-read-the-data"&gt;Step 4: Read the data&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram compares the two ways to read the data: a console-only path through Cost Explorer, and a SQL path over the CUR 2.0 export with Amazon Athena.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-2.png" alt="Two reporting paths, the Cost Explorer console view and a SQL query over the CUR 2.0 export in Amazon Athena" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Reporting with Cost Explorer compared to CUR 2.0 and Amazon Athena&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Console path, no custom code.&lt;/strong&gt; Cost Explorer, filtered to Amazon Bedrock and grouped by your tag key, gives per-team or per-user spend as a chart and table. This covers most recurring reporting needs and requires no query authoring.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;SQL path, low code.&lt;/strong&gt; Point Amazon Athena at the CUR 2.0 export to group spend by &lt;code&gt;iamPrincipal/team&lt;/code&gt; and usage type:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
    tags['iamPrincipal/team'] AS team,
    line_item_usage_type,
    SUM(line_item_unblended_cost) AS cost
FROM cur2_view
WHERE product_servicecode IN ('AmazonBedrock', 'AmazonBedrockService')
AND billing_period = '2026-09'
GROUP BY 1, 2
ORDER BY 3 DESC&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Things to note:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Service code&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Bedrock spend can be recorded under more than one &lt;code&gt;product_servicecode&lt;/code&gt; value, for example, &lt;code&gt;AmazonBedrock&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;AmazonBedrockService&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Table name&lt;/strong&gt;: The table name (&lt;code&gt;cur2_view&lt;/code&gt;) is whatever the setup script from Step 3 named it when it created the Glue table. Check the script or your Glue catalog if it differs. Column names match the CUR 2.0 Parquet schema regardless of table name.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Billing-period column.&lt;/strong&gt; Confirm how your export exposes the period. Some exports use &lt;code&gt;billing_period&lt;/code&gt; (YYYY-MM). Others partition on a start-date or year/month column.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Two notes worth carrying into any query you write.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Count all token types&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Bedrock bills four token types, each at a different rate: input, output, cache read, and cache write. Each is a separate line item with its own usage type and unit price, so summing only input and output undercounts. The gap is largest for workloads that use prompt caching heavily. Refer to the AWS document &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-understanding-cur-data.html#cost-mgmt-cur-pricing" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Understanding Amazon Bedrock pricing in CUR&lt;/a&gt; for the latest information.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Match the rate to the line item, not the model&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon Bedrock pricing in CUR is determined by three things: the token type, the service tier, and whether the request was routed through cross-Region inference. In-Region and cross-Region requests are priced differently, and service tier is reflected in the usage type as well. The usage type carries a suffix indicating cross-Region routing and service tier, so if your workloads mix these, match the rate to each line item rather than applying one rate per model. Refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-understanding-cur-data.html#cost-mgmt-cur-usage-type-examples" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CUR usage type examples&lt;/a&gt; for examples.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-add-enforcement-with-aws-budgets-and-budget-actions"&gt;Step 5: Add enforcement with AWS Budgets and budget actions&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Budgets performs the attach, so there is no custom code for this workflow. Budget actions attach to a specific IAM user, group, or role. This isolates one person’s access only if that person already has their own role. If your Amazon Bedrock traffic runs through a role that many people share, a Deny action attached to that role blocks everyone on it, not only the person who crossed the threshold. The following diagram explains the enforcement workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21962-3.png" alt="AWS Budgets threshold triggering a budget action that attaches a Deny IAM policy" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Enforcement with AWS Budgets and budget actions&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Budgets can run an action when a threshold is crossed. Available actions include applying a custom IAM policy, applying a service control policy (SCP), and targeting Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) or Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) instances. For this use case, the IAM policy action is the relevant one.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;First, create the Deny policy the action will attach. This example denies the higher-cost models while leaving a lower-cost model available, so the user can keep working:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": [
        "bedrock:InvokeModel",
        "bedrock:InvokeModelWithResponseStream"
      ],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:bedrock:*::foundation-model/[HIGHER-COST-MODEL-ID]"
      ]
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Replace &lt;code&gt;[HIGHER-COST-MODEL-ID]&lt;/code&gt; with the model ID you want to deny in your environment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Then set up permissions and the budget itself.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;There are two distinct roles here, and the AWS managed policy only covers one of them:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Your own user or role&lt;/strong&gt; (the one configuring the budget) needs the AWS managed policy &lt;code&gt;AWSBudgetsActionsWithAWSResourceControlAccess&lt;/code&gt;. It grants budgets permissions plus the conditioned &lt;code&gt;iam:PassRole&lt;/code&gt; needed to hand your budget action role to AWS Budgets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The IAM role AWS Budgets assumes to perform the action&lt;/strong&gt; is not covered by a single named managed policy for this use case. Create it with a trust policy that allows the principal &lt;code&gt;budgets.amazonaws.com&lt;/code&gt; to assume it (scoped with &lt;code&gt;aws:SourceArn&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;aws:SourceAccount&lt;/code&gt; conditions), and a permissions policy granting &lt;code&gt;iam:AttachRolePolicy&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;iam:DetachRolePolicy&lt;/code&gt; (and &lt;code&gt;organizations:AttachPolicy&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;organizations:DetachPolicy&lt;/code&gt; if you are also using SCPs). Refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cost-management/latest/userguide/budgets-action-role.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Setting up a role for AWS Budgets to run budget actions&lt;/a&gt; for the grantable permissions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create the budget action role described earlier.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create a cost budget scoped to Amazon Bedrock, filtered to the identity or tag value you want to cap.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;In &lt;strong&gt;Configure alerts&lt;/strong&gt;, add an alert threshold. Choose &lt;strong&gt;Actual&lt;/strong&gt; for realized spend or &lt;strong&gt;Forecasted&lt;/strong&gt; to act before the spend lands.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Under &lt;strong&gt;Attach actions&lt;/strong&gt;, choose &lt;strong&gt;Add Action&lt;/strong&gt;, select the budget actions role, and choose the IAM policy action. Select the Deny policy and the IAM user, group, or role to attach it to.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For automatic enforcement, choose &lt;strong&gt;Yes&lt;/strong&gt; to run the action automatically. Choose &lt;strong&gt;No&lt;/strong&gt; to require a human to approve each action from the &lt;strong&gt;Alert details&lt;/strong&gt; page.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Configure at least one notification so the affected user is told why access changed.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can attach multiple actions to a single threshold, and multiple thresholds to one budget. That is how you build tiers: for example, deny the premium model at 80 percent and the mid-tier model at 100 percent, while never denying the low-cost model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Only the management account can apply SCPs, and from the management account you can apply an SCP to another account.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="considerations"&gt;Considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Budgets is not near-real-time&lt;/strong&gt;: Budget data refreshes up to three times a day, so spend can overshoot a threshold before the action runs.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost allocation tags are not retroactive&lt;/strong&gt;: Expect up to 24 hours for a newly applied tag key to appear, then up to another 24 hours after you activate it before it begins populating billing data.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;CUR does not contain per-request line items&lt;/strong&gt;: Both classic CUR and CUR 2.0 aggregate Amazon Bedrock cost by usage type, operation, and pricing/resource attributes over an hour or a day.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;IAM principal fields require CUR 2.0&lt;/strong&gt;: The legacy CUR format does not include them. Per-token cost breakdowns are available in both formats.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Session tags are bound at AssumeRole time, not per request.&lt;/strong&gt; Their values are constant for every call made with that session’s credentials, and they surface only as aggregated billing data. Session tags are not written to model invocation logs. Logs capture the caller’s &lt;code&gt;identity.arn&lt;/code&gt;. To distinguish users on a shared session at the request level, use a per-user &lt;code&gt;RoleSessionName&lt;/code&gt;, or set the user in request metadata on each call.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Budget actions attach to IAM users, groups, and roles.&lt;/strong&gt; If your users are federated and have no standing IAM identity, the action needs a role to target. This is why the pattern works best when each person or application has its own role. If your users share a role or are federated without their own role, see our previous &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/tokenomics-at-scale-how-jamf-built-real-time-spend-enforcement-for-amazon-bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;post&lt;/a&gt; for a per-user enforcement approach.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;A shared gateway role collapses attribution by default.&lt;/strong&gt; With a large language model (LLM) gateway, the identity captured is the gateway’s execution role.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;New models need attention.&lt;/strong&gt; Usage types are model-specific. When you enable a new model, confirm it appears in your queries and budget filters as expected rather than assuming it is covered.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="decision-framework"&gt;Decision framework&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Requirement&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Recommended path&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quick reporting&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost Explorer grouped by identity&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-team reporting&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost Explorer with activated tags (Steps 1, 2, and 4)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Detailed line-item analysis&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;CUR 2.0 export (Steps 1 through 4)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Enforcement&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS Budgets with budget actions (Steps 1 and 5)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cleanup"&gt;Cleanup&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete all the resources you created.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learned how to get per-identity Amazon Bedrock cost visibility and enforceable spend limits using only AWS-native features, with no application code to write for the common case. As generative AI adoption scales, the organizations that win will be the ones that can answer the tokenomics question with confidence: what do we spend per person or per team, can we cap it, and how much work does it take to get there.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Now, it’s your turn to start exploring. We are here to help, and if you need further assistance, reach out to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/contact-us/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Support&lt;/a&gt; and your AWS account team.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Optimizing cost per task and proving agent ROI on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/optimizing-cost-per-task-and-proving-agent-roi-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Majd Arbash]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 18:43:19 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">8db53e7d13c7fb7eb110840d4bdf22942b447b95</guid>

					<description>Comparing per-token prices hides the real cost of running a model on Amazon Bedrock. This post shows how to calculate the true cost per task across four hidden cost drivers, reduce it with caching, routing, and output constraints, and prove agent ROI with human baselines, holdout experiments, and a five-dimension scorecard.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/managing-amazon-bedrock-costs-end-to-end-a-playbook-for-finops-and-platform-teams/"&gt;Part 1&lt;/a&gt; of this series built the operating model that controls &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; spend: attribution with AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) principal tags and Cost and Usage Report 2.0, observability with model invocation logging, enforcement at three tiers, and a review cadence. That layer answers who spent it, and you can stop them. It can’t tell you whether the spend was well spent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post answers two questions that Part 1 deliberately left open. First: Why is this workflow expensive? The answer requires thinking in cost per task, not cost per token. Second: Is the agent better than the humans were? The answer requires baselines, controlled experiments, and a scorecard tied to decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learn a framework for calculating true model cost (including four hidden cost drivers that the pricing page doesn’t show), techniques for reducing cost per task through caching, routing, and output constraints, and a discipline for measuring agent ROI with human baselines, holdout experiments, and a five-dimension scorecard. This post is written for two audiences: the application and platform engineers who own the optimization levers, and the product owners and FinOps partners who need to justify agent spend to leadership.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="cost-per-task-not-cost-per-token"&gt;Cost per task, not cost per token&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Price per token invites quick comparison, but it doesn’t reflect what you actually pay. The published rate reflects the cost of a single token. Your bill reflects the cost of completing a task. A task is one complete unit of work your application asks a model to do: resolve one support email, extract fields from one invoice, or answer one user question. A task runs from the request through to a usable result, however many model calls it takes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The lower-priced model might not be the cheaper one to run. A cheaper model might fragment your text into more tokens, pad responses with filler, misread your tool schema and loop through corrections, or burn thousands of reasoning tokens before answering. None of these costs appear on the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-four-hidden-cost-drivers"&gt;The four hidden cost drivers&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four model characteristics determine what that published rate actually costs you on your workload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="tokenizer-efficiency"&gt;Tokenizer efficiency&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A token isn’t a word, and it is not equal across models. Every model family uses a different tokenizer and vocabulary. The same text, code, or JSON payload splits into different token counts. If Model A’s list price is 15 percent lower but its tokenizer needs 40 percent more tokens for your data (common with structured schemas and numerical arrays), Model B is cheaper. The gap widens for non-English text: many tokenizers fragment non-Latin scripts into two to three times more tokens than the equivalent English.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="output-alignment-and-verbosity"&gt;Output alignment and verbosity&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Output tokens commonly cost 3–5x more than input tokens (the ratio varies by family. Refer to the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt;). A model that ignores formatting constraints like “return only raw JSON” makes you pay that output premium for filler words. A model that follows formatting instructions closely saves money by generating fewer of these more expensive output tokens.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="agentic-loop-efficiency"&gt;Agentic loop efficiency&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Agentic workflows change the cost math entirely. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/tool-use.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Tool use (function calling)&lt;/a&gt; lets a model invoke external functions, requiring a multi-turn loop: analyze the prompt, decide to call a tool, wait for the observation, generate an answer. Each turn re-sends the full conversation history and tool results as input tokens. A model that takes four turns (each one re-sending a larger context payload) costs far more than one that finishes in a single pass.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="reasoning-overhead"&gt;Reasoning overhead&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Many models support &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-reasoning.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;extended thinking&lt;/a&gt;, working through a chain of thought before the visible answer. Reasoning tokens bill as output tokens, so a model that thinks for 2,000 tokens before a 200-token answer bills you for 2,200. Some models redact the reasoning while still charging for it. Start with reasoning turned off and enable it only when benchmarks show a quality gap that justifies the cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="cost-per-task-a-worked-example"&gt;Cost per task: A worked example&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-21540-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-21540-1.png" alt="Radar chart of five cost dimensions where Model A leads only on price advantage and Model B leads on the other four" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Comparing two models across five cost dimensions. List price is only one of five factors that determine actual cost&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider an agentic workflow that reads a 750-word customer email, extracts entities, calls an API to verify account status, and outputs strict JSON. The following comparison uses illustrative prices (always benchmark against your own data and current &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt;):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model A (lower list price)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model B (higher list price)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Input list price&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0.50 / 1M tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$1.00 / 1M tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Output list price&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$1.50 / 1M tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$3.00 / 1M tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tokenizer efficiency (turn 1 input)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,500 tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,000 tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Output verbosity (final response)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;450 tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;150 tokens&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Agentic loops required&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3 turns (error correction)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1 turn (zero-shot)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cumulative input tokens, all turns&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;6,150&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1,000&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cumulative output tokens, all turns&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;750&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;150&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Total task cost&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0.0042&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;$0.00145&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model A: (6,150 x $0.50 + 750 x $1.50) / 1,000,000 = $0.0042&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model B: (1,000 x $1.00 + 150 x $3.00) / 1,000,000 = $0.00145&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-21540-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/02/ML-21540-2.png" alt="Flowchart: Model A takes three correction turns re-sending context ($0.0042); Model B finishes in one turn ($0.00145)" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Why the higher-priced model costs 65% less. Each extra turn re-sends a growing context payload, compounding the bill&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Why the 2x-priced model costs 65 percent less:&lt;/strong&gt; three compounding factors. Model A’s tokenizer fragments the email into 50 percent more tokens, inflating every input payload. It pads its JSON with filler, tripling output tokens. And it misaligns with the tool schema, needing two correction turns that each re-send a growing history.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="cost-per-successful-task"&gt;Cost per successful task&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The preceding example assumes both models eventually succeed. In production they won’t, and a failure is rarely free. The formulas:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For retry-until-success workflows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;cost per successful task = cost per attempt / success rate&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For escalate-on-failure workflows:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;code&gt;cost per successful task = cost per attempt + (1 - success rate) x escalation cost&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Input further splits into three buckets when &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;prompt caching&lt;/a&gt; is active: uncached input at base rate, cache reads below base rate, and cache writes above base rate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="measuring-it-on-your-own-workload"&gt;Measuring it on your own workload&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Which model is actually cheaper only shows up when you benchmark against your own data. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/evaluation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock model evaluation&lt;/a&gt; is the managed feature for the quality half of that: define criteria, upload your prompt datasets, and get scored results with automatic metrics and optional human review, with no custom infrastructure required.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Quality scores alone don’t produce a bill, though. Three more Bedrock features supply the token and turn counts you price against them:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/APIReference/API_runtime_CountTokens.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;CountTokens&lt;/a&gt; returns the input token count a given model would charge for a given payload without running inference. It’s the cheapest way to compare tokenizer efficiency (driver #1) across candidates before you spend anything on generation.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Model invocation response carries a &lt;code&gt;usage&lt;/code&gt; block with &lt;code&gt;inputTokens&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;outputTokens&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;cacheReadInputTokens&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;cacheWriteInputTokens&lt;/code&gt;. Accumulating those across the turns of a single task gives you the four terms of the preceding cost-per-attempt formula. One gotcha: when caching is enabled, &lt;code&gt;inputTokens&lt;/code&gt; counts only the uncached tokens, so your total input volume is &lt;code&gt;inputTokens + cacheReadInputTokens + cacheWriteInputTokens&lt;/code&gt;. Sum them to know the footprint, but price them separately, since the three bill at different rates. Evaluate this multiple times throughout your journey and set the cache checkpoints based on what is the active current load. The drift in cache checkpoints happens as the application progresses and the tools become stale. This means that you will be writing more to the cache in 3 months from now when the drift has happened. Evaluating your data constantly for optimal cache usage becomes a primary lever to reduce cost.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Model invocation logging&lt;/a&gt; writes per-invocation token counts to Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) or Amazon CloudWatch Logs, so you can measure turns-to-completion against real production traffic instead of a sample. Tag calls with &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-request-metadata.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;request metadata&lt;/a&gt; to slice those logged token counts by team, feature, or experiment. However, these are log fields, not cost allocation tags, and never appear in AWS Cost Explorer or your Cost and Usage Report. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/cost-mgmt-application-inference-profiles.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Application inference profiles&lt;/a&gt; do reach both, at a grain of one usage type per day. That grain is right for chargeback but too coarse for cost per task. Multiplying logged token counts by the published rates gives you a near real-time estimate rather than an invoice. It won’t reflect commitments, batch pricing, or provisioned throughput unless you model them. Reconcile against your Cost and Usage Report before you treat it as the bill.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="reducing-cost-per-task-four-levers"&gt;Reducing cost per task: Four levers&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each of the four drivers has a lever, and each is documented in depth. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Prompt caching&lt;/a&gt; cuts the re-sent context that made Model A expensive, which makes it the largest win on agentic paths. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-routing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Intelligent prompt routing&lt;/a&gt; sends the easy requests to a cheaper model in the same family. A &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/tool-use.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;tool schema&lt;/a&gt; plus a maxTokens cap holds verbosity down more reliably than prose instructions do, and extended thinking stays off until a benchmark shows it earns its cost back. &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/batch-inference.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Batch inference&lt;/a&gt; halves the rate on bulk single-turn work, though it rules out tool calling and multi-turn exchange.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="proving-agent-roi-describe-the-human-baseline"&gt;Proving agent ROI: Describe the human baseline&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Efficient isn’t the same as valuable. An agent can be perfectly optimized and still not be worth running. The question that decides budgets is: is the agent better than the humans were?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="document-the-human-process-first"&gt;Document the human process first&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The biggest mistake in agent ROI is trying to prove value without first describing the human workflow. Before building or scaling an agent, write down the current process in operational terms: what triggers the work, which human roles are involved, where the handoffs sit, how completion is defined. This sounds like bureaucracy. It’s actually measurement design. Every instrumentation decision downstream (what counts as a run, what counts as success, where human time is spent) falls out of this description.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="measure-the-baseline-before-the-agent-ships"&gt;Measure the baseline before the agent ships&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With the process described, measure the human baseline over a meaningful window: time to completion, queue time, active effort, reopen rate, escalation rate, cost per task, and outcome quality. Baselines must be measured before the agent goes live, not reconstructed from memory after the fact. Retrospective baselines are systematically flattering to the agent: people remember the average case and forget the fast ones.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="instrument-the-workflow-as-an-experiment"&gt;Instrument the workflow as an experiment&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After the baseline exists, instrument the agent workflow as an experiment from day one. Every run gets a trace ID, and every meaningful step is observable. A workflow-run record aggregates everything above the model call into a single analyzable unit:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "runId": "run-8f3a2c",
  "workflow": "renewal-risk-summary",
  "agent": "renewal-agent",
  "principalArn": "arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/BedrockRuntimeRole/gw-alex",
  "team": "sales-ai",
  "costCenter": "CC-2041",
  "experiment": {
    "variant": "treatment",
    "holdout": False,
    "promptVersion": "v14",
    "policyVersion": "2026-07"
  },
  "ops": {
    "latencyMs": 41200,
    "retries": 1,
    "toolCalls": 6,
    "humanApprovals": 1,
    "humanMinutes": 35,
    "escalations": 0
  },
  "econ": {
    "inputTokens": 48210,
    "outputTokens": 3105,
    "cacheReadTokens": 31500,
    "estCostUsd": 0.87
  },
  "outcome": {
    "status": "success",
    "accepted": True,
    "reopened": False,
    "revenueEventId": "opp-44121"
  }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three fields deserve emphasis:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;experiment block:&lt;/strong&gt; Stamped at run creation and propagated into Bedrock &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/APIReference/API_runtime_Converse.html#bedrock-runtime_Converse-request-requestMetadata" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;request metadata&lt;/a&gt;, so model-level and workflow-level records join cleanly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;humanMinutes:&lt;/strong&gt; What most teams omit and most need. Without it, “the agent did the work” silently excludes the 35 minutes a human spent reviewing it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;revenueEventId:&lt;/strong&gt; The hook that makes revenue attribution possible later. It costs nothing to record and can’t be reconstructed afterward.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The rule: If the workflow telemetry can’t answer the business question, the instrumentation is incomplete.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="join-cost-to-outcomes"&gt;Join cost to outcomes&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;invocation logs&lt;/a&gt; landing in Amazon S3 (Part 1) and workflow-run records in your analytics store, one join produces the metrics that matter. Amazon Bedrock logs record tokens, not dollars, so first build a view that prices each invocation from token counts and your model rate sheet, aggregated by run ID. Then query with &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/athena/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Athena&lt;/a&gt;:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-sql"&gt;SELECT
  w.workflow,
  w.variant,
  COUNT(*) AS runs,
  SUM(IF(w.outcome_status = 'success', 1, 0)) AS successes,
  ROUND(SUM(c.est_cost_usd) /
    NULLIF(SUM(IF(w.outcome_status = 'success', 1, 0)), 0), 2)
    AS cost_per_success,
  ROUND(SUM(w.retries) * 1.0 /
    NULLIF(SUM(IF(w.outcome_status = 'success', 1, 0)), 0), 2)
    AS retries_per_success,
  ROUND(SUM(w.human_minutes) /
    NULLIF(SUM(IF(w.outcome_status = 'success', 1, 0)), 0), 1)
    AS human_min_per_success
FROM workflow_runs w
JOIN invocation_costs_by_run c ON c.run_id = w.run_id
GROUP BY w.workflow, w.variant
ORDER BY cost_per_success DESC&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; Column names and join keys shown in the preceding query are illustrative. Verify against your invocation log schema and workflow telemetry store, which might vary by implementation.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cost per successful outcome, retries per success, and human minutes per success are the three numbers that survive contact with a skeptical CFO. Cost per call flatters agents that loop. Cost per success doesn’t. Note the variant grouping: the same query, split by experiment arm, is the ROI comparison.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="compare-agents-and-humans-correctly"&gt;Compare agents and humans correctly&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The most credible evaluation is an A/B or holdout test. Controlled treatment and control groups compare the agent-assisted workflow against the human baseline without confusing the result with market, seasonal, or organizational changes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For customer-facing or revenue-linked workflows, randomize at the visitor, lead, account, or case level. For internal workflows (support operations, coding assistants), use matched cohorts: teams, repository groups, or pods.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The comparison covers four dimensions:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Efficiency:&lt;/strong&gt; time to completion, hours recovered, queue-time reduction.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Quality:&lt;/strong&gt; error rate, approval rate, reopen rate, escalation rate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost:&lt;/strong&gt; cost per task, cost per successful outcome, total cost to serve.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Business value:&lt;/strong&gt; conversion lift, retention lift, expansion rate, revenue per rep or per workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If the agent improves one dimension while meaningfully degrading another, the result is ambiguous and the deployment needs tuning.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="revenue-the-hardest-metric"&gt;Revenue: The hardest metric&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Efficiency gains are the easiest part of the ROI story to prove. Revenue impact is harder, and it is the metric leadership funds against. In revenue-adjacent workflows, measure whether the agent improves conversion, time-to-response, deal progression, or revenue per rep. Holdout tests are the most reliable way to separate incremental revenue attributable to the agent from general business momentum.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When revenue lift is real, the conversation changes. The organization stops asking whether the Bedrock bill went up and starts asking whether the additional spend is buying a meaningful increase in business output.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-scorecard"&gt;The scorecard&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Based on what we have observed working with customers, a strong operating model doesn’t require fifty metrics. What works is a small scorecard tied to decisions, reviewed on a regular cadence for every production agent.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following thresholds are starting points, not prescriptions. Calibrate them against your own fleet data and organizational context.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dimension&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Green&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Yellow&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Red&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Adoption&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Active users / intended users&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&amp;gt;70%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;40-70%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&amp;lt;40%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Quality&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Tasks completed without rework&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&amp;gt;90%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;75-90%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&amp;lt;75%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Efficiency&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Hours recovered per week&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&amp;gt;target&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;50-100% of target&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&amp;lt;50% of target&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cost per successful outcome&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;≤budget&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;1-1.5x budget&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&amp;gt;1.5x budget&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Business impact&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;KPI lift compared to baseline&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Significant&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Marginal&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;None or negative&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Green means scale: the agent beats baseline while meeting cost and quality thresholds. Yellow means tune: promising but needs work. Red means retire or redesign: the agent consumes cost without enough measurable value. The willingness to actually retire a red agent is what makes the rest of the scorecard credible.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you learned how to calculate the true cost of a model by accounting for four hidden cost drivers (tokenizer efficiency, output alignment, agentic loop efficiency, and reasoning overhead), how to reduce cost per task through prompt caching, model routing, output constraints, and batch inference, and how to prove agent ROI by measuring human baselines, instrumenting workflows as experiments, joining cost to outcomes, and running holdout comparisons.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The cost optimization half answers “why is this expensive and how do I fix it.” The ROI half answers “is the agent better than the humans were.” Together with Part 1’s operating model (attribute, observe, enforce, optimize, operate), they form a complete framework: you can see who spent it, explain why, reduce the waste, and justify what remains.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, benchmark your top model candidates against your own prompts using &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/evaluation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock model evaluation&lt;/a&gt;, capture tokens and turns from the &lt;code&gt;usage&lt;/code&gt; block or your &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-invocation-logging.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;invocation logs&lt;/a&gt;, price them against current &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt;, and divide by success rate. For ROI measurement, consult the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/wellarchitected/latest/agentic-ai-lens/agentcost07.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Well-Architected Agentic AI Lens&lt;/a&gt; for agent cost governance best practices.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you have found other techniques for reducing cost per task or measuring agent ROI on Amazon Bedrock, let us know in the comments.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;
