[22:54:53] INFO PASS E12: all seven trust boundary checks passed; each token works only where it's trusted
[22:54:53] INFO done in 1.4s&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In that run, the report covered two production accounts and six findings, with the escalation contacts for each account. You can send the same request from any HTTP client, without an A2A SDK.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="production-considerations"&gt;Production considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This demo simplifies several areas for clarity. For production, consider these changes:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;One runtime per customer, or a front door that accepts several customer identity providers, with per-customer callback clients.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Key-based client authentication (private_key_jwt or mutual TLS) instead of client secrets.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS PrivateLink for private connectivity, and TLS inside the VPC for end-to-end encryption.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Customer managed AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) keys for Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3), Amazon ECR, AWS Secrets Manager, Amazon CloudWatch Logs, and Amazon Bedrock Guardrails.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;VPC Flow Logs and Network Load Balancer access logs. Both API Gateway stages already write access logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A Secrets Manager rotation function for the Cognito client credentials.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An authenticated onboarding portal or API instead of files.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If calls through API Gateway intermittently return HTTP 503, check that cross-zone load balancing is on for the internal Network Load Balancer. Each ECS service runs a single task that can start in either Availability Zone.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The repository’s README covers the full list in Security controls and Considerations for production.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="security-considerations"&gt;Security considerations&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Deploy this solution only in sandbox accounts with no customer or business data, and delete the stacks after testing. The sample uses synthetic data only, with no personal data. Its compliance agent checks example internal policies. It doesn’t assess or certify compliance with any law, regulation, or standard. The sample includes the following controls. You’re responsible for reviewing and adapting them before you use this pattern in production:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Transport encryption. Every cross-account call uses HTTPS to Amazon API Gateway or Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and every route except the context agent’s public Agent Card requires a Cognito JWT.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Guardrails. In this sample, each model call uses an Amazon Bedrock guardrail with prompt-attack and harmful-content filters, and text from the other organization is checked against the same guardrail before a model uses it.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Least-privilege IAM. Each workload has its own role that names exact ARNs. Only the orchestrator and the compliance agent can read a secret, each only its own.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Container hardening. ECS containers run with a read-only root filesystem and all Linux capabilities dropped. The base image is pinned by digest.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Account isolation. The two accounts share no secret, key, or IAM permission. They exchange only public values.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up-resources"&gt;Clean up resources&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete all resources when you have finished testing. Run the Clean up section at the bottom of notebook 3, then notebook 2, one cell at a time. The last cell in each section confirms that nothing is left. After cleanup, check the AWS CloudFormation, Amazon ECR, and Amazon Cognito consoles in both accounts, and the Amazon Bedrock AgentCore console in the SaaS provider account, to confirm that no resources remain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This post showed how to build a multi-agent system where agents from different organizations, built on different frameworks, collaborate through A2A on AWS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A LangChain orchestrator on Amazon ECS communicates with a Strands agent on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. Neither side needs the other’s framework or SDK. The SaaS agent calls back to the enterprise context agent for account metadata, and each side authenticates with its own identity provider. A new skill from the same ISV requires no changes to the code. Adding a second ISV takes one extension, because the orchestrator holds a single OAuth client today: give it one client and scope per ISV, as the README’s Considerations for production describes. Then run the same onboarding exchange, add its Agent Card URL, and restart the orchestrator.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-a2a-cross-org-agents" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; and run the three notebooks, in two accounts, with Jupyter on your computer or on Amazon SageMaker AI notebook instances. They handle infrastructure provisioning, agent deployment, and end-to-end testing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To learn more about the services and protocol in this post, see the following resources:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/what-is-bedrock-agentcore.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Developer Guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/what-is-bedrock.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://strandsagents.com" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Strands Agents SDK documentation&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/opensource/open-protocols-for-agent-interoperability-part-4-inter-agent-communication-on-a2a/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Open Protocols for Agent Interoperability Part 4: Inter-Agent Communication on A2A&lt;/a&gt;&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If you have questions or feedback, leave them in the comments section.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Fine-grained access control for Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base tools</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/fine-grained-access-control-for-amazon-bedrock-managed-knowledge-base-tools/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Renya Kujirada]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 12 Nov 2026 15:40:24 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Bases]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">2144423e5e4caeacf910447d8e0923290056c400</guid>

					<description>Learn how to expose an Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base to AI agents as MCP tools through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway while keeping per-user access control. A request interceptor injects verified identity, Policy in AgentCore validates it independently, and a resource-based policy restricts direct access to the knowledge base.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;More enterprises are building AI agents into their daily work, and a common pattern is an agent that searches internal documents to compose its answers. A single organization might soon run hundreds of agents, each connected to many data sources and tools. This raises a question. When an agent searches on behalf of a user, can you make sure that it uses only the information that user is allowed to see?&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Consider AnyCompany, a fictional company that builds an internal assistant shared by all employees. An employee in department A asks, “Compare my department’s business plan with the notice to all departments, and list the initiatives that are behind schedule.” The agent writes its own search queries and keeps searching internal documents until it has enough information. Employees in department B ask the same assistant the same question. If the application doesn’t scope searches to each user, the department B business plan can end up in an answer to an employee in department A. If you replace the departments with customer companies, the same problem occurs in a multi-tenant software as a service (SaaS) architecture where multiple customers share one assistant and knowledge base.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Controlling access to multi-tenant data through this agent is harder than in traditional applications. In a traditional application, the application code determines who the signed-in user is and searches with that user’s permissions. In an agent, a large language model (LLM) decides at runtime which tool to call and with which arguments. If a search tool takes “who to search as” as an argument, the LLM decides that value too. A prompt injection or an LLM error might then make the agent search as a different employee.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we build an internal assistant for AnyCompany. All employees share the same agent and knowledge base, yet each employee can search only the documents they’re allowed to view. We use &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-build-managed.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base&lt;/a&gt; as the search backend and expose it to the agent as Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools through &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway&lt;/a&gt;, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore. The design principle is to not let the LLM decide who the user is. Instead, trusted code decides, based on information that your identity provider has verified. On top of that, we add independent layers of defense in case that mechanism has a bug or is bypassed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Managed Knowledge Base has a feature called &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-managed-acl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ACL awareness&lt;/a&gt;. When a search request includes a user context (&lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt;) with the user’s email in &lt;code&gt;userId&lt;/code&gt;, the knowledge base returns only the documents that user is allowed to view. If your application calls the search API directly, the application code sets the search scope from the authentication result. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-multi-tenant-agentic-chat-applications-on-enterprise-data-with-amazon-bedrock-managed-knowledge-base/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Build multi-tenant agentic chat applications on enterprise data with Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base&lt;/a&gt; shows this pattern. When you expose the knowledge base as an MCP tool through AgentCore Gateway, however, &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; becomes a tool argument, and the LLM constructs its value. Neither the gateway nor the knowledge base verifies that this value belongs to the authenticated user.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This solution injects verified user attributes into tool calls at the tool layer (AgentCore Gateway). It then scopes the search by those attributes at the resource layer (Managed Knowledge Base). The sample stores three documents in a single knowledge base: business plans for department A and department B, and a notice shared with all departments. Two users, user-a in department A and user-b in department B, search through the same agent. The following diagram shows the architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-1.png" alt="Request flow from the agent through the AgentCore Gateway interceptor and policy to the Managed Knowledge Base with ACL-aware retrieval" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: Solution architecture&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The request flow is as follows. The step numbers correspond to the numbers in Figure 1. Figure 1 omits the call from the web app to AgentCore runtime, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, so its step numbers differ from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-managed-knowledge-base-patterns/blob/main/agentcore-access-control/README.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;diagram in the repository README&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user signs in with Amazon Cognito and receives an access token, a JSON Web Token (JWT). A pre token generation AWS Lambda trigger adds an &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; claim to it. The agent forwards this token in the &lt;code&gt;Authorization&lt;/code&gt; header when it sends MCP requests to the gateway.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The JWT authorizer of the gateway validates the signature, expiration, and client ID of the token.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A request interceptor (an AWS Lambda function) on the gateway resolves the email from the validated token and overwrites the &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; argument of &lt;code&gt;tools/call&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Policy in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore (Policy in AgentCore) evaluates the modified request against a Cedar policy. It denies the request if &lt;code&gt;userContext.userId&lt;/code&gt; doesn’t match the &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; claim in the JWT.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The connector target calls the Managed Knowledge Base search API with the execution role of the gateway. The resource-based policy of the knowledge base denies searches from principals other than this role.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;With ACL awareness, the knowledge base returns only chunks from documents that the &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; user is allowed to view.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The four mechanisms map to layers and lines of defense as follows.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tool layer (AgentCore Gateway)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resource layer (Managed Knowledge Base)&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Primary defense&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Request interceptor: injects &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; deterministically from the verified token, which helps prevent the LLM or the client from spoofing it&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ACL awareness: returns only the documents that the &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; user is allowed to view&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Secondary defense&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Policy in AgentCore: checks the injected &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; against the token and denies requests that don’t match&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Resource-based policy: denies search requests from principals other than the gateway execution role&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For requests that go through the gateway, the two primary defenses are enough to achieve fine-grained access control, as long as no other principal can call the knowledge base. ACL awareness filters results based on the &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; that it receives and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-managed-ds-s3-acl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;isn’t an authorization mechanism by itself&lt;/a&gt;. The two secondary defenses cover cases where an assumption behind the primary defenses fails, such as an interceptor bug or a principal that calls the knowledge base without going through the gateway. Add them based on your requirements. If other principals in the account have &lt;code&gt;bedrock:Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; permissions, add the resource-based policy. The rest of this post covers ACL awareness, the MCP tools for Managed Knowledge Base, the request interceptor, Policy in AgentCore, and the resource-based policy, in that order.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account and AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) permissions to create the resources in this post.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS Region where Managed Knowledge Base, AgentCore Gateway, Policy in AgentCore, and AgentCore runtime are available.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Access to the Amazon Bedrock model that the agent uses.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;code&gt;Node.js&lt;/code&gt; 22 or later and npm.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.astral.sh/uv/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;uv&lt;/a&gt;, to package the agent and run the verification script.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;If you adapt the pattern to an existing Amazon Cognito user pool, the user pool must be on the Essentials or Plus feature plan, which access token customization requires. The stack in this post creates a user pool on the Essentials plan.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can deploy the architecture in Figure 1 with a single AWS Cloud Development Kit (AWS CDK) stack from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-managed-knowledge-base-patterns/tree/main/agentcore-access-control" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;agentcore-access-control directory&lt;/a&gt; of the sample-managed-knowledge-base-patterns repository. Run the commands in this post from that directory. The repository installs the AWS CDK CLI and aws-cdk-lib (2.274.0 or later) as npm dependencies. The stack also deploys a web app on Amazon CloudFront and runs the agent, built with Strands Agents, on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime. By default, the agent uses Claude Sonnet 5.5 through the global cross-Region inference profile &lt;code&gt;global.anthropic.claude-sonnet-5-5&lt;/code&gt;. The agent forwards the access token of the signed-in user to the gateway and contains no access control logic. For deployment steps, including how to fetch only this directory, see the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-managed-knowledge-base-patterns/blob/main/agentcore-access-control/README.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;README&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="scope-searches-per-user-with-acl-awareness"&gt;Scope searches per user with ACL awareness&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Managed Knowledge Base is a new type of Amazon Bedrock Knowledge Base. You connect a data source, and it runs parsing, chunking, embedding, indexing, search, and reranking as a fully managed service. It provides two search APIs. &lt;code&gt;Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; runs a hybrid search. &lt;code&gt;AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt; breaks a query into subqueries and searches multiple knowledge bases iteratively.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ACL awareness keeps the access permissions of the data source at ingestion time. At search time, it returns only the documents that the user can view. For an Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) data source, you &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-managed-ds-s3-acl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;define ACLs&lt;/a&gt; either in a global ACL file per S3 prefix or in a metadata file per document. The sample in this post uses metadata files. When you place the following metadata file at the same S3 path as the document, only user-a can retrieve &lt;code&gt;dept-a-plan.txt&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "metadataAttributes": { "department": "d001", "year": 2026 },
  "accessControlList": [
    { "Name": "user-a@example.com", "Type": "USER", "Access": "ALLOW" }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you adapt the sample, keep these behaviors of ACL-enabled Amazon S3 data sources in mind. A document without an ACL entry isn’t ingested. A change to the global ACL file requires reindexing the affected prefix, while per-document metadata files reindex only the affected documents and take precedence over the global file. Amazon S3 has no real-time ACL verification, so an ACL change applies after the next sync. ACL mistakes don’t return errors. The affected documents are silently left out of the results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;At query time, pass the user’s email in the top-level &lt;code&gt;userContext.userId&lt;/code&gt; for both &lt;code&gt;Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt;. Queries without &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; don’t return documents from a data source that has ACLs enabled. In contrast, documents from a data source that doesn’t have ACLs enabled are returned to all users, with or without &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;response = client.retrieve(
    knowledgeBaseId=kb_id,
    retrievalQuery={"text": "What is the plan management code in the department A business plan?"},
    userContext={"userId": "user-a@example.com"},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the ACLs that the knowledge base keeps for the three sample documents at ingestion, and the search results for each &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-2.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-2.png" alt="Knowledge base ACLs for three documents at ingestion and the filtered results returned for each user’s userContext" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 2: Scoping searches with ACL awareness&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Managed Knowledge Base doesn’t authenticate end users. It trusts the &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; that it receives and filters results based on it. Passing a verified &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; is the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-managed-acl.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;responsibility of the caller&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="expose-managed-knowledge-base-as-mcp-tools"&gt;Expose Managed Knowledge Base as MCP tools&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When you create a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-target-connector-managed-kb.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Managed Knowledge Base connector target&lt;/a&gt; on AgentCore Gateway, you can expose two MCP tools: &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;target-name&amp;gt;___Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;&amp;lt;target-name&amp;gt;___AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt;. The administrator fixes the target knowledge base in &lt;code&gt;parameterValues&lt;/code&gt;. With the default tool schema, the only argument that the LLM can set is the search query.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To use ACL awareness, expose &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; in the tool schema with &lt;code&gt;parameterOverrides&lt;/code&gt; when you create the target. A connector target has no tool code of your own, so a tool argument is the only way to pass &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; to the knowledge base, and only &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-add-target-api-target-config.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;the fields that you expose with parameterOverrides&lt;/a&gt; can be set as tool arguments. The interceptor that we describe later also sets &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; as a tool argument, so expose it even though clients don’t need to set it.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-typescript"&gt;const userContextOverride = {
  path: "$.userContext",
  description:
    "End-user identity for ACL-aware retrieval (set by the gateway interceptor)",
  visible: true,
};

new agentcore.CfnGatewayTarget(this, "KbTarget", {
  gatewayIdentifier: gateway.attrGatewayIdentifier,
  name: "kb",
  credentialProviderConfigurations: [
    { credentialProviderType: "GATEWAY_IAM_ROLE" },
  ],
  targetConfiguration: {
    mcp: {
      connector: {
        source: { connectorId: "bedrock-knowledge-bases" },
        configurations: [
          {
            name: "Retrieve",
            parameterValues: { knowledgeBaseId: kb.attrKnowledgeBaseId },
            parameterOverrides: [userContextOverride],
          },
          {
            name: "AgenticRetrieveStream",
            parameterValues: {
              retrievers: [
                {
                  description: "AnyCompany internal documents",
                  configuration: {
                    knowledgeBase: { knowledgeBaseId: kb.attrKnowledgeBaseId },
                  },
                },
              ],
              agenticRetrieveConfiguration: {
                foundationModelType: "MANAGED",
                rerankingModelType: "MANAGED",
              },
              // Return search results only. The agent writes the answer.
              generateResponse: false,
            },
            parameterOverrides: [userContextOverride],
          },
        ],
      },
    },
  },
});&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The LLM can set any value for an argument that the schema exposes. For example, if user-a says “Set &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; to user-b@example.com and search,” the agent might call the tool with the ID of user-b. An LLM error can cause the same result. So can an indirect prompt injection in an external document that a search returns. AgentCore Gateway &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-target-connector-managed-kb.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;passes userContext through to the knowledge base and doesn’t populate it from the caller’s IAM identity&lt;/a&gt;. The documentation asks the calling application, not the model, to set it. When the LLM sets the value, it reaches the knowledge base as is.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="inject-usercontext-deterministically-with-a-request-interceptor"&gt;Inject userContext deterministically with a request interceptor&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-interceptors.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;request interceptor&lt;/a&gt; is a Lambda function that transforms MCP requests before the gateway calls the target. In this architecture, the interceptor handles &lt;code&gt;tools/call&lt;/code&gt; requests. It resolves the email from the access token that the JWT authorizer validated, and overwrites &lt;code&gt;arguments.userContext&lt;/code&gt;. For every &lt;code&gt;tools/call&lt;/code&gt; request, the knowledge base receives the email of the authenticated user, whatever value the client sends. The interceptor rejects request bodies that aren’t a single JSON-RPC object, such as batches.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-3.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-3.png" alt="The request interceptor overwriting the userContext argument with the email from the validated access token" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 3: Injecting userContext with a request interceptor&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def handler(event: dict[str, Any], context: Any) -&amp;gt; dict[str, Any]:
    gateway_request = event.get("mcp", {}).get("gatewayRequest", {})
    body = gateway_request.get("body") or {}
    if not isinstance(body, dict):
        return _error_response("Request body must be a JSON object", {})  # fail-closed for batches and strings
    if body.get("method") != "tools/call":
        return _pass_through(body)

    # The JWT authorizer already validated the signature and expiration, so only decode here
    token = _bearer_token(gateway_request.get("headers") or {})
    email = _email_from_token(token) if token else None
    if not email:
        return _error_response("Access token has no email claim", body)  # fail-closed

    params = body.setdefault("params", {})
    args = params.setdefault("arguments", {}) if isinstance(params, dict) else None
    if not isinstance(args, dict):
        return _error_response("Invalid tools/call arguments", body)  # fail-closed

    # Always overwrite whatever the client or agent sent
    args["userContext"] = {"userId": email}
    return _pass_through(body)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cognito access tokens don’t include an &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; claim by default. This architecture adds the email to the access token with a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/user-pool-lambda-pre-token-generation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;pre token generation Lambda trigger&lt;/a&gt; (event version V2_0), only when the email is verified. Access token customization with event version V2_0 requires the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/user-pool-lambda-pre-token-generation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Essentials or Plus feature plan&lt;/a&gt;, so the sample creates the user pool on the Essentials plan. On a Lite plan user pool, the access token has no &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; claim, and the interceptor and Policy in AgentCore deny every tool call. Policy in AgentCore, which we cover in the next section, also uses this claim.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Without the trigger, the interceptor can get the email from the Cognito &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito-user-identity-pools/latest/APIReference/API_GetUser.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GetUser&lt;/a&gt; API, the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/userinfo-endpoint.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;userInfo endpoint&lt;/a&gt;, or an ID token that the client sends in a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-headers.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;separate header&lt;/a&gt;. Each option has its own scope or validation requirements. The &lt;code&gt;GetUser&lt;/code&gt; API requires the &lt;code&gt;aws.cognito.signin.user.admin&lt;/code&gt; scope, which also lets the token call user self-service APIs. The userInfo endpoint requires the &lt;code&gt;openid&lt;/code&gt; scope, so it doesn’t accept tokens from user pool API sign-in, such as &lt;code&gt;ADMIN_USER_PASSWORD_AUTH&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/inbound-jwt-authorizer.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;JWT authorizer&lt;/a&gt; of the gateway validates only the bearer token in the &lt;code&gt;Authorization&lt;/code&gt; header, so the interceptor must verify the ID token itself: its signature, &lt;code&gt;iss&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;aud&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;token_use&lt;/code&gt;, expiration, and &lt;code&gt;email_verified&lt;/code&gt;, and that its &lt;code&gt;sub&lt;/code&gt; matches the access token. Cognito ID tokens carry &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; whether or not it’s verified. All three methods leave the access token without an &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; claim. The policy in the next section then has nothing to compare with and denies every call, so use these methods only if you don’t add Policy in AgentCore.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With another identity provider (IdP), such as Microsoft Entra ID, you can add &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; to access tokens as an optional claim for both defenses to reference. However, Microsoft doesn’t guarantee that this value is correct or immutable and &lt;a href="https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/entra/identity-platform/optional-claims-reference" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;advises against using it for authorization&lt;/a&gt;. For ACL matching, your IdP or application must verify the association between the authenticated user and their email within trusted tenants. Update ACLs and sync the data source when email addresses change or are reassigned.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;ACL matching uses an exact email match, so your identity provider must keep each email unique and unchanged. For this reason, the sample user pool disables self sign-up and makes the email attribute immutable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You attach the interceptor in &lt;code&gt;interceptorConfigurations&lt;/code&gt; of the gateway. With &lt;code&gt;passRequestHeaders: true&lt;/code&gt;, the gateway passes the &lt;code&gt;Authorization&lt;/code&gt; header that the JWT authorizer validated to the interceptor. The headers that the gateway passes include the bearer token, so &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/gateway-interceptors.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;make sure that the interceptor doesn’t log them&lt;/a&gt;. The sample logs only the tool name, whether the client supplied &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt;, and whether the interceptor replaced it, not the event, the headers, or the token.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-typescript"&gt;const gateway = new agentcore.CfnGateway(this, "Gateway", {
  name: "managed-kb-gateway",
  roleArn: gwRole.roleArn,
  protocolType: "MCP",
  authorizerType: "CUSTOM_JWT",
  authorizerConfiguration: {
    customJwtAuthorizer: { discoveryUrl, allowedClients: [userPoolClientId] },
  },
  interceptorConfigurations: [
    {
      interceptionPoints: ["REQUEST"],
      interceptor: { lambda: { arn: interceptorFn.functionArn } },
      inputConfiguration: { passRequestHeaders: true },
    },
  ],
});&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cognito &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/amazon-cognito-user-pools-using-the-access-token.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;access tokens&lt;/a&gt; carry the app client ID in &lt;code&gt;client_id&lt;/code&gt; and include &lt;code&gt;aud&lt;/code&gt; only when the client requests &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cognito/latest/developerguide/cognito-user-pools-define-resource-servers.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;resource binding&lt;/a&gt;. The JWT authorizer &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/inbound-jwt-authorizer.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;checks allowedClients against client_id and allowedAudience against aud&lt;/a&gt;, and verifies both when you set both. So set only &lt;code&gt;allowedClients&lt;/code&gt; to the app client ID. With resource binding, you can also set &lt;code&gt;allowedAudience&lt;/code&gt; to the resource URI in &lt;code&gt;aud&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The MCP client only attaches the access token to the &lt;code&gt;Authorization&lt;/code&gt; header. It doesn’t need code that handles &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt;. In the sample, the agent on AgentCore runtime forwards the access token that it received from the web app.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In the sample, AgentCore runtime and the gateway accept the same access token, and the agent forwards it. If your requirements call for tokens that only one resource accepts, request them with resource binding and set &lt;code&gt;allowedAudience&lt;/code&gt; on each authorizer. Resource binding is available only with managed login.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="validate-the-injected-request-with-policy-in-agentcore"&gt;Validate the injected request with Policy in AgentCore&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With only the interceptor, access control depends on a single component. In the following cases, the gateway accepts a request with an invalid &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; as is:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A bug in the interceptor injects a wrong value or skips the overwrite.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An unauthorized code change injects the value of another user.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A configuration change detaches the interceptor from the gateway.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Policy in AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; evaluates tool calls to the gateway with Cedar policies. It &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/secure-ai-agents-with-policy-and-lambda-interceptors-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;runs after the request interceptor&lt;/a&gt;. It can reference the claims of the token that the JWT authorizer validated as tags on the principal. The following policy permits a call only when the injected &lt;code&gt;userContext.userId&lt;/code&gt; matches the &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; claim in the JWT. The policy engine denies calls that don’t meet this condition.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-cedar"&gt;permit(
  principal is AgentCore::OAuthUser,
  action == AgentCore::Action::"kb___Retrieve",
  resource == AgentCore::Gateway::"&amp;lt;GATEWAY_ARN&amp;gt;"
) when {
  principal.hasTag("email") &amp;amp;&amp;amp;
  context has input &amp;amp;&amp;amp; context.input has userContext &amp;amp;&amp;amp;
  context.input.userContext has userId &amp;amp;&amp;amp;
  context.input.userContext.userId == principal.getTag("email")
};&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this policy, &lt;code&gt;principal&lt;/code&gt; represents the authenticated user, and &lt;code&gt;resource&lt;/code&gt; specifies the gateway ARN. The action &lt;code&gt;kb___Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; refers to the &lt;code&gt;Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; tool on the gateway target named &lt;code&gt;kb&lt;/code&gt;. The &lt;code&gt;when&lt;/code&gt; clause requires the tool argument &lt;code&gt;userContext.userId&lt;/code&gt; to match the JWT’s &lt;code&gt;email&lt;/code&gt; claim. The snippet shows the policy for &lt;code&gt;kb___Retrieve&lt;/code&gt;. The sample creates a second policy with the same condition for &lt;code&gt;kb___AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt;, because each policy names a single action and &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/example-policies-action-resource.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;the policy engine denies any request that no permit policy matches&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figure shows how Policy in AgentCore denies a tool call when the interceptor doesn’t overwrite &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; correctly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-4.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-4.png" alt="Policy in AgentCore denying a tool call when the injected userContext does not match the token’s email claim" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 4: Validating the injected request with Policy in AgentCore&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The interceptor and the policy both use the email from the same token, but they read it from different sources. The policy takes the expected value from the signature-verified token, and the actual value from the request body that the interceptor built. This lets it detect a missing interceptor or a wrong injection. The IdP, the pre token generation trigger, and the policy configuration itself remain shared dependencies of both. With the policy alone, the LLM goes back to constructing &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt;, so we recommend that you combine injection and validation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You attach the policy engine that holds the Cedar policy in &lt;code&gt;policyEngineConfiguration&lt;/code&gt; of the gateway. When you set &lt;code&gt;mode&lt;/code&gt; to &lt;code&gt;ENFORCE&lt;/code&gt;, the gateway denies requests based on the evaluation result. When you add policies to a gateway that’s already in use, you can start with &lt;code&gt;LOG_ONLY&lt;/code&gt;, check the decisions in the traces, and then switch to &lt;code&gt;ENFORCE&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-typescript"&gt;const gateway = new agentcore.CfnGateway(this, "Gateway", {
  // Authorizer and interceptor settings are the same as in the earlier example
  policyEngineConfiguration: {
    arn: policyEngine.attrPolicyEngineArn,
    mode: "ENFORCE",
  },
});&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Create the gateway target before creating the Cedar policies. Creating the target registers its tool schema with the policy engine. Policy creation &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-create-update-validation.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;fails&lt;/a&gt; if a policy references a tool that has not yet been registered, whatever the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock-agentcore/latest/devguide/policy-validation-overview.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;validationMode&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="restrict-access-to-the-knowledge-base-with-a-resource-based-policy"&gt;Restrict access to the knowledge base with a resource-based policy&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Policy in AgentCore evaluates only requests that go through a gateway with the policy engine attached. Two paths bypass the tool-layer controls. One is another gateway without the interceptor and the policy that points to the same knowledge base. The other is a direct call to the &lt;code&gt;Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; API from an SDK or the AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-5.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-5.png" alt="A resource-based policy denying Retrieve calls from principals other than the gateway execution role" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 5: Restricting callers with a resource-based policy&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Managed Knowledge Base supports &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-managed-cross-account.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;resource-based policies&lt;/a&gt;. When you attach the following policy to the knowledge base, it explicitly denies &lt;code&gt;Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; and &lt;code&gt;GetDocumentContent&lt;/code&gt; to principals other than the gateway execution role. Agentic retrieval can call &lt;code&gt;GetDocumentContent&lt;/code&gt; to expand results to full documents. An explicit deny takes precedence over an allow in an identity-based policy in the same account, so the deny also applies to administrators and to other consumers of the knowledge base, such as the test window on the console. If other roles need to search the knowledge base, add their Amazon Resource Names (ARNs) to &lt;code&gt;ArnNotEquals&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "DenyRetrieveExceptGatewayRole",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Principal": "*",
      "Action": ["bedrock:Retrieve", "bedrock:GetDocumentContent"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:bedrock:&amp;lt;REGION&amp;gt;:&amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt;:knowledge-base/&amp;lt;KB_ID&amp;gt;",
      "Condition": {
        "ArnNotEquals": {
          "aws:PrincipalArn": "arn:aws:iam::&amp;lt;ACCOUNT_ID&amp;gt;:role/&amp;lt;GATEWAY_EXECUTION_ROLE&amp;gt;"
        }
      }
    }
  ]
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;With AWS CDK, you can attach this policy document with &lt;code&gt;CfnKnowledgeBasePolicy&lt;/code&gt;, which maps to the &lt;code&gt;AWS::Bedrock::KnowledgeBasePolicy&lt;/code&gt; resource type. Set &lt;code&gt;aws:PrincipalArn&lt;/code&gt; to the ARN of the gateway execution role, for example &lt;code&gt;gwRole.roleArn&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can’t list &lt;code&gt;bedrock:AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt; in the &lt;code&gt;Action&lt;/code&gt; element of the policy. However, &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-test-agentic-retrieve.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;the searches inside AgenticRetrieveStream authorize bedrock:Retrieve&lt;/a&gt; for the caller on each knowledge base. As a result, the deny in this policy also blocks searches through &lt;code&gt;AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A resource-based policy limits which IAM principals can search the knowledge base. It can’t deny another gateway that reuses the same execution role. Don’t share the execution role with other workloads. Restrict which users can pass the role (&lt;code&gt;iam:PassRole&lt;/code&gt;) and manage the resource-based policy (&lt;code&gt;bedrock:PutResourcePolicy&lt;/code&gt;, &lt;code&gt;bedrock:GetResourcePolicy&lt;/code&gt;, and &lt;code&gt;bedrock:DeleteResourcePolicy&lt;/code&gt;), because anyone who can change or delete the policy can remove this control. The policy also doesn’t cover control plane operations such as &lt;code&gt;GetKnowledgeBase&lt;/code&gt;, so protect the knowledge base configuration with identity-based policies.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="test-the-solution"&gt;Test the solution&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-managed-knowledge-base-patterns/blob/main/agentcore-access-control/scripts/verify_defenses.py" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;scripts/verify_defenses.py&lt;/a&gt; script in the repository signs in as both users and confirms that each mechanism blocks the path that it targets. Each sample document contains a unique code, so the script can tell from a response whether a document was inadvertently disclosed. The script signs in as the demo users with &lt;code&gt;ADMIN_USER_PASSWORD_AUTH&lt;/code&gt;. For the AWS permissions that it needs, see &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-managed-knowledge-base-patterns/blob/main/agentcore-access-control/docs/verification.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;docs/verification.md&lt;/a&gt;. The agent scenario depends on the model, so the script reports it as inconclusive (&lt;code&gt;SKIP&lt;/code&gt;) if the agent doesn’t send the spoofed &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; in three turns.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To check Policy in AgentCore and the resource-based policy on their own, deploy the stack with &lt;code&gt;-c enableBypassSimulation=true&lt;/code&gt;. This flag adds two test gateways for the same knowledge base. One has no interceptor, and the other has neither an interceptor nor a policy engine. Both run as a test role that the resource-based policy doesn’t exempt. Redeploy without the flag after testing to remove them. The following table shows the results.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Scenario&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Result&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Blocked by&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;user-a and user-b send the same query&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Each user gets only the documents in their ACL&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;ACL awareness&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;user-a calls &lt;code&gt;kb___Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; or &lt;code&gt;kb___AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt; with the &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; of user-b&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Only the documents user-a can view return&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Request interceptor&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;user-a prompts the agent to set the &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; of user-b&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The agent might set it. If it does, only the documents user-a can view return&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Request interceptor&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A client sends the &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; of another user to a gateway without an interceptor&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The gateway denies the tool call with a &lt;code&gt;Tool Execution Denied&lt;/code&gt; error&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Policy in AgentCore&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A separate gateway with no interceptor, no policy, and its own execution role searches the knowledge base&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The tool returns an error because the knowledge base denies the search&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Resource-based policy&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A user with administrator permissions calls &lt;code&gt;Retrieve&lt;/code&gt; directly from an SDK&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;AccessDeniedException&lt;/code&gt; with an explicit deny in a resource-based policy&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Resource-based policy&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A user with administrator permissions calls &lt;code&gt;AgenticRetrieveStream&lt;/code&gt; directly from an SDK&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The stream fails with a &lt;code&gt;dependencyFailedException&lt;/code&gt; that wraps the explicit deny on &lt;code&gt;bedrock:Retrieve&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Resource-based policy&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When user-a and user-b send the opening question, the answer for user-a draws on the department A business plan and the notice, and the answer for user-b draws on the department B business plan and the notice.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The web app shows the tool arguments that the LLM generated next to the documents that came back. When user-a asks the agent to search as user-b, the app shows the spoofed &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt;, but only the documents of user-a appear.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following screenshot shows the web app after user-a asks the agent to search as user-b.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-6.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/11/21865-6.png" alt="The web app showing the spoofed userContext in the tool arguments while only user-a’s documents return" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 6: The interceptor overrides the spoofed userContext&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid future charges, delete the stack when you finish testing. Run &lt;code&gt;npx cdk destroy&lt;/code&gt; in the &lt;code&gt;infra&lt;/code&gt; directory of the pattern. This deletes the knowledge base, the S3 buckets and their objects, the user pool, the demo user password in AWS Secrets Manager, and the Amazon CloudWatch Logs log groups of every Lambda function and of the agent runtime.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how to share an agent and a knowledge base across a company and still limit each user’s searches to the documents they’re allowed to view. As the primary defense, a request interceptor injects &lt;code&gt;userContext&lt;/code&gt; from the verified token, and ACL awareness in Managed Knowledge Base scopes the search. Based on your requirements, Policy in AgentCore validates the injected value independently, and a resource-based policy denies searches that bypass the gateway.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The underlying design principle is to not let the LLM generate values that affect authorization. This principle also applies to a multi-tenant SaaS architecture where multiple customers share a knowledge base. It also applies to MCP tools other than Managed Knowledge Base that take arguments affecting access control. Deploy the CDK stack from the &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws-samples/sample-managed-knowledge-base-patterns/tree/main/agentcore-access-control" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;GitHub repository&lt;/a&gt; and try it with your own data sources and IdP. To learn when to use interceptors and when to use policies, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/secure-ai-agents-with-policy-and-lambda-interceptors-in-amazon-bedrock-agentcore-gateway" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Secure AI agents with Policy and Lambda interceptors in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore gateway&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture in this post uses the built-in AgentCore Gateway connector for Managed Knowledge Base. If you’re considering migrating from a &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/kb-build-managed.html#kb-managed-vs-custom" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Customer-managed Knowledge Base&lt;/a&gt; to a Managed Knowledge Base, the &lt;code&gt;amazon-bedrock&lt;/code&gt; skill in the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/products/developer-tools/agent-toolkit-for-aws/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Agent Toolkit for AWS&lt;/a&gt; includes a &lt;a href="https://github.com/aws/agent-toolkit-for-aws/blob/main/skills/core-skills/amazon-bedrock/references/migrate-knowledge-base-to-managed.md" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;migration procedure&lt;/a&gt; to help you evaluate and plan the transition. It works in coding agents that you already use, such as Kiro, Claude Code, and Cursor. The agent reads your current configuration with read-only calls and writes a migration plan, optionally with a cost comparison based on your measured usage. After you approve the plan, it creates the new knowledge base next to the original and doesn’t change or delete the original. For the benefits of Managed Knowledge Base and a step-by-step migration walkthrough, see &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/migrate-to-amazon-bedrock-managed-knowledge-base-with-your-coding-agent/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Migrate to Amazon Bedrock Managed Knowledge Base with your coding agent&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;
