			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Automating AWS Agent Registry updates with push-based architecture</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/automating-aws-agent-registry-updates-with-push-based-architecture/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Prachi Kulkarni]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 11 Nov 2026 20:52:48 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock AgentCore]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">eee5d43fead189e55bac9d295c979e1d24fbb7ff</guid>

					<description>This post shows how to keep the AWS Agent Registry current automatically. Using AWS CloudTrail, Amazon EventBridge, AWS Lambda, and AgentCore Identity, an event-driven pipeline detects MCP server changes and pushes updated tool metadata to the registry with no stored secrets, across single- and multi-account environments.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Every time a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server published in the AWS Agent Registry, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, gains a new tool or updates its schema, the registry record must be updated too. Without automated push-based sync, consumers discover stale metadata. For organizations managing multiple MCP servers across accounts, such a sync keeps catalog records current within an existing governance workflow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In most enterprise deployments, a catalog’s value depends on its freshness. If a developer deploys a new tool on Monday, but the registry record still reflects last week’s schema, consumers lose trust in the catalog. Stale metadata directly reduces registry reliability, leading teams to inadvertently build dependencies on inaccurate information.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When an engineer adds a tool to an MCP server but forgets to update the catalog:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Consumers miss out on the new capability entirely.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Applications might call the tool using an outdated schema, causing unexpected runtime behavior or silent workflow deviations.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Multiplied across teams, this lack of automation erodes trust in the registry. Organizations ultimately fall back on ad hoc discovery mechanisms like hardcoded endpoints, informal knowledge sharing, and stale documentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we outline an event-driven architecture that automates these updates using AWS CloudTrail, Amazon EventBridge, and AWS Lambda.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following solution brings these services together into an event-driven pipeline that detects runtime changes and synchronizes them to the registry automatically.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture supports both multi-account and single-account environments:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Single-account setup: For straightforward deployments, all components reside within Account A. Cross-account forwarding setup isn’t needed as events flow directly within Account A.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Multi-account setup: This involves two accounts where runtime events from Account B are securely forwarded to Account A. 
  &lt;ul&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Account A (registry account)&lt;/strong&gt; owns the AWS Agent Registry, the Amazon EventBridge rule, the push-sync Lambda function, and CloudTrail logs.&lt;/li&gt; 
   &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Account B (MCP server account)&lt;/strong&gt; hosts the MCP server runtime and the Amazon Cognito OAuth provider used for authentication.&lt;/li&gt; 
  &lt;/ul&gt; &lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;The sample uses two accounts, but you can scale the architecture for multiple accounts, with Account A as the registry account and multiple AWS accounts similar to Account B for different MCP servers.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the end-to-end event flow for an update to an MCP server in Account B. The captured event is forwarded to Account A, where a Lambda function queries the MCP server for its tools and updates the matching registry record.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21088-1-1.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ML-21088-1-1.png" alt="Architecture diagram of the end-to-end event flow: an MCP server update in Account B is captured by AWS CloudTrail, forwarded through Amazon EventBridge to a Lambda function in Account A, which queries the MCP server and updates the matching AWS Agent Registry record." width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: End-to-end event flow for a cross-account MCP server update, from the CloudTrail event in Account B to the registry record update in Account A.&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The architecture uses five AWS services:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS CloudTrail&lt;/strong&gt; captures &lt;code&gt;UpdateAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt; API calls as management events.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon EventBridge&lt;/strong&gt; routes matching events to the Lambda function, with cross-account forwarding supported.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/strong&gt; queries the MCP server for its current tools, diffs against the registry, and updates the record if changed.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AWS Agent Registry&lt;/strong&gt; stores MCP server records and their tool schemas as the central source of truth.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Identity, a capability of Amazon Bedrock AgentCore,&lt;/strong&gt; brokers OAuth token exchanges securely, so the Lambda authenticates without storing secrets.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-it-works-business-logic-flow"&gt;How it works: business logic flow&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After it’s deployed, the pipeline runs the following end-to-end workflow:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Trigger&lt;/strong&gt;: A developer in &lt;strong&gt;Account B&lt;/strong&gt; updates an MCP server, which calls the &lt;code&gt;UpdateAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt; API in Amazon Bedrock AgentCore, and AWS CloudTrail captures the event.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Route&lt;/strong&gt;: Amazon EventBridge catches the CloudTrail event and forwards it to the central event bus in &lt;strong&gt;Account A&lt;/strong&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Parse&lt;/strong&gt;: The sync Lambda in &lt;strong&gt;Account A&lt;/strong&gt; extracts the runtime Amazon Resource Name (ARN) from the event payload and dynamically constructs the MCP server’s invocation URL. This is how it knows which server in &lt;strong&gt;Account B&lt;/strong&gt; to query.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Authenticate&lt;/strong&gt;: The Lambda generates a secure OAuth bearer token through AgentCore Identity.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Fetch&lt;/strong&gt;: The Lambda calls the MCP server (&lt;code&gt;initialize&lt;/code&gt;, then &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt;) to retrieve the latest tools information.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Sync&lt;/strong&gt;: The Lambda finds the matching registry record by its ARN, evaluates the differences, and updates the registry if the schema has changed.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This flow assumes that the Agent Registry in Account A already contains a pre-registered record for the MCP server (created during initial onboarding). The Lambda function in this sample updates existing records only. It doesn’t generate new ones.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This same flow works in a single-account setup where all resources (registry, Lambda, Amazon EventBridge rule, and MCP server) reside in one account. In that case, skip the cross-account forwarding step. Events flow directly within the same account’s Amazon EventBridge bus.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="technical-walkthrough"&gt;Technical walkthrough&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This section walks through the workflow for a single MCP server update with two accounts as depicted in the architecture diagram.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The same flow executes independently for each server that emits an &lt;code&gt;UpdateAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt; event. The Lambda handles one event at a time, scaling naturally with the Amazon EventBridge invocation model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-1-event-capture-and-routing-for-cross-account-setup"&gt;Step 1: Event capture and routing for cross-account setup&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When a developer deploys or updates an MCP server, the &lt;code&gt;UpdateAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt; API is called, either by the AgentCore CLI (&lt;code&gt;agentcore deploy&lt;/code&gt;) or directly.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS CloudTrail records this as a management event in Account B, with a delivery delay of approximately 5–15 minutes. The event is then delivered to Account A’s default Amazon EventBridge bus.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a cross-account setup, Account B’s Amazon EventBridge forwards matching events to Account A’s event bus through a cross-account rule.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon EventBridge rule target in Account A invokes the sync Lambda, passing the full CloudTrail event JSON as the input payload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Amazon EventBridge rule pattern:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-json"&gt;{
  "source": ["aws.bedrock-agentcore"],
  "detail-type": ["API Call via AWS CloudTrail"],
  "detail": { "eventName": ["UpdateAgentRuntime"] }
}&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-2-extract-the-mcp-server-url"&gt;Step 2: Extract the MCP server URL&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Lambda parses the runtime ARN from the event. It extracts the hosting Account ID and AWS Region to dynamically construct the target MCP server’s URL-encoded invocation endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Extract the runtime ARN from the AWS CloudTrail event payload
def _extract_mcp_url(event):
    detail = event.get("detail", {})
    runtime_arn = detail.get("responseElements", {}).get("agentRuntimeArn", "")
    if not runtime_arn:
        return None, None
    # Parse the account ID from the ARN (5th colon-separated segment)
    arn_parts = runtime_arn.split(":")
    account_id = arn_parts[4] if len(arn_parts) &amp;gt; 4 else None
    region = detail.get("awsRegion", "us-west-2")
    # URL-encode the ARN and build the MCP invocation endpoint
    encoded_arn = runtime_arn.replace(":", "%3A").replace("/", "%2F")
    mcp_url = (f"https://bedrock-agentcore.{region}.amazonaws.com"
               f"/runtimes/{encoded_arn}/invocations?qualifier=DEFAULT")
    return mcp_url, account_id&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-3-secure-authentication-with-agentcore-identity"&gt;Step 3: Secure authentication with AgentCore Identity&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Lambda authenticates using a two-step identity flow. It exchanges its execution identity for a temporary workload token. The Lambda then trades this token for a scoped OAuth bearer token through the target account’s credential provider.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def get_bearer_token(account_id=None):
    """Get OAuth bearer token via AgentCore Identity credential provider.

    Two-step process:
    1. Get a workload access token from AgentCore Identity (identifies this Lambda)
    2. Use it to fetch an OAuth token from the credential provider (M2M flow)

    The credential provider stores the Cognito/OAuth config securely in AgentCore
    Identity, so no client secrets are needed in Lambda env vars.
    """
    acct = account_id or ""
    provider_name = os.environ.get(f"CREDENTIAL_PROVIDER_{acct}") or os.environ.get("CREDENTIAL_PROVIDER", "")
    scope_str = os.environ.get(f"CREDENTIAL_SCOPE_{acct}") or os.environ.get("CREDENTIAL_SCOPE", "")
    scopes = [s.strip() for s in scope_str.split(",") if s.strip()] if scope_str else []
    workload_name = os.environ.get("WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_NAME", "")
    if not provider_name:
        raise ValueError(f"No CREDENTIAL_PROVIDER configured for account {acct}")
    if not workload_name:
        raise ValueError("WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_NAME env var not set")
    region = os.environ.get("AWS_REGION", "us-west-2")
    client = boto3.client("bedrock-agentcore", region_name=region)
    # Step 1: Get workload access token (identifies this Lambda as a trusted workload)
    wat_response = client.get_workload_access_token(
        workloadName=workload_name,
    )
    print(f"Workload access token response keys: {list(wat_response.keys())}")
    workload_token = wat_response.get("workloadAccessToken") or wat_response.get("accessToken", "")
    if not workload_token:
        raise ValueError(f"No access token in workload response: {list(wat_response.keys())}")
    # Step 2: Use workload token to get OAuth token from the credential provider
    response = client.get_resource_oauth2_token(
        workloadIdentityToken=workload_token,
        resourceCredentialProviderName=provider_name,
        oauth2Flow="M2M",
        scopes=scopes,
    )
    return response["accessToken"]&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Credential providers are per-account (&lt;code&gt;CREDENTIAL_PROVIDER_{ACCT_ID}&lt;/code&gt;), so one Lambda authenticates MCP servers across multiple accounts.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-4-query-the-mcp-server"&gt;Step 4: Query the MCP server&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Using standard MCP streamable-HTTP mechanics, the Lambda establishes an active session through an &lt;code&gt;initialize&lt;/code&gt; request. After the session is active, it calls &lt;code&gt;tools/list&lt;/code&gt; to fetch the complete array of currently exposed tools and schemas.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;def call_tools_list(mcp_url, token):
    """Call the MCP server's initialize + tools/list methods and return the result.

    MCP streamable-http requires initialize before tools/list.
    The session_id from initialize is passed to tools/list if the server uses sessions.
    """
    # Validate URL scheme to prevent file:// or custom scheme access
    if not mcp_url.startswith("https://"):
        raise ValueError(f"Only HTTPS URLs are allowed, got: {mcp_url[:50]}")
    # Step 1: Initialize MCP session
    init_payload = {
        "jsonrpc": "2.0",
        "id": "init-1",
        "method": "initialize",
        "params": {
            "protocolVersion": "2025-03-26",
            "capabilities": {},
            "clientInfo": {"name": "tool-sync-lambda", "version": "1.0.0"},
        },
    }
    init_resp = requests.post(
        mcp_url,
        json=init_payload,
        headers=_mcp_headers(token),
        timeout=30,
    )
    init_resp.raise_for_status()
    session_id = init_resp.headers.get("Mcp-Session-Id")
    init_result = _parse_sse_json(init_resp.text)
    print(f"MCP session initialized, session_id={session_id}")
    # Step 2: Call tools/list
    list_payload = {
        "jsonrpc": "2.0",
        "id": "list-1",
        "method": "tools/list",
        "params": {},
    }
    list_resp = requests.post(
        mcp_url,
        json=list_payload,
        headers=_mcp_headers(token, session_id),
        timeout=30,
    )
    list_resp.raise_for_status()
    return _parse_sse_json(list_resp.text)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-5-find-the-matching-registry-record"&gt;Step 5: Find the matching registry record&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Lambda queries the central Agent Registry to locate the corresponding record. It searches APPROVED records for one whose &lt;code&gt;mcpServer.data&lt;/code&gt; contains the runtime ARN embedded during record creation (see the Deployment section).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Decode the runtime ARN from the MCP URL for matching
def _find_record_by_mcp_url(client, registry_id, mcp_url):
    """Search registry records to find one whose server schema contains the runtime ARN.

    Tries three matching strategies:
    1. Decoded ARN (e.g. arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:...:runtime/TrimMCP-xxx)
    2. URL-encoded ARN (e.g. arn%3Aaws%3Abedrock-agentcore%3A...%2FTrimMCP-xxx)
    3. Full MCP URL match

    Returns:
        (record_id, full_record) tuple. Both None if no match found.
    """
    # Extract the runtime ARN from the MCP URL for matching
    # URL format: .../runtimes/arn%3A...%2Fruntime%2F&amp;lt;id&amp;gt;/invocations...
    # Decode to get: arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:region:account:runtime/id
    try:
        decoded_url = urllib.parse.unquote(mcp_url)
        # Extract just the runtime path: arn:aws:bedrock-agentcore:...:runtime/xxx
        runtime_marker = "/runtimes/"
        idx = decoded_url.find(runtime_marker)
        if idx &amp;gt;= 0:
            runtime_arn = decoded_url[idx + len(runtime_marker) :].split("/invocations")[0]
        else:
            runtime_arn = None
    except Exception:  # noqa: BLE001
        runtime_arn = None
    records = client.list_registry_records(registryId=registry_id)
    record_list = records.get("registryRecords", [])
    print(f"Found {len(record_list)} registry records")
    for rec in record_list:
        record_id = rec.get("registryRecordId") or rec.get("recordId") or rec.get("id", "")
        record_name = rec.get("name", "?")
        record_status = rec.get("status", "?")
        print(f" Record: {record_id} | {record_name} | status={record_status} | keys={list(rec.keys())}")
        if not record_id:
            print(f" Warning: could not get record ID from: {list(rec.keys())}")
            continue
        if record_status == "DRAFT":
            print(f" Skipping DRAFT record {record_id} ({record_name}) — must be APPROVED first")
            continue
        try:
            full = client.get_registry_record(
                registryId=registry_id,
                recordId=record_id,
            )
            descriptors = full.get("descriptors", {})
            mcp_desc = descriptors.get("mcpServer", {})
            inline = mcp_desc.get("data", "")
            # Match on runtime ARN (decoded or encoded) in the server schema
            if runtime_arn and runtime_arn in inline:
                print(f"Found matching record (by ARN): {record_id} ({rec.get('name', '?')})")
                return record_id, full
            # Also check URL-encoded ARN
            encoded_arn = runtime_arn.replace(":", "%3A").replace("/", "%2F") if runtime_arn else None
            if encoded_arn and encoded_arn in inline:
                print(f"Found matching record (by encoded ARN): {record_id} ({rec.get('name', '?')})")
                return record_id, full
            if mcp_url in inline or urllib.parse.unquote(mcp_url).replace("?qualifier=DEFAULT", "") in inline:
                print(f"Found matching record (by URL): {record_id} ({rec.get('name', '?')})")
                return record_id, full
        except Exception as e:  # noqa: BLE001
            print(f"Error checking record {record_id}: {e}")
            continue
    print(f"No matching record found among {len(record_list)} records.")
    print(f" Looking for runtime ARN: {runtime_arn}")
    print(f" Looking for MCP URL: {mcp_url}")
    return None, None&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="step-6-diff-and-update"&gt;Step 6: Diff and update&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To minimize unnecessary writes, tool lists are normalized (sorted by name, compared using name + description + inputSchema). The registry update only fires when a change is detected. If the schemas differ, the Lambda logs the exact additions or removals and pushes the fresh definitions to the registry record.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Normalize tool definitions for comparison (sort by name, extract key fields)
def _normalize_tools(tools):
    """Normalize tool list for comparison — extract name, description, inputSchema."""
    normalized = []
    for t in sorted(tools, key=lambda x: x.get("name", "")):
        normalized.append(
            {
                "name": t.get("name", ""),
                "description": t.get("description", ""),
                "inputSchema": t.get("inputSchema", {}),
            }
        )
    return normalized

def _normalize_tools(tools):
    """Normalize tool list for comparison — extract name, description, inputSchema."""
    normalized = []
    for t in sorted(tools, key=lambda x: x.get("name", "")):
        normalized.append(
            {
                "name": t.get("name", ""),
                "description": t.get("description", ""),
                "inputSchema": t.get("inputSchema", {}),
            }
        )
    return normalized

def sync_registry_if_changed(mcp_tools, mcp_url):
    """Compare MCP server tools with AWS Agent Registry record tools. Update only if different.

    Steps:
    1. Find the AWS Agent Registry record matching this MCP server's URL
    2. Extract existing tools from the record's tools.inlineContent
    3. Normalize both tool lists (sort by name, compare name/description/inputSchema)
    4. If identical → skip update
    5. If different → log the diff and update the registry record

    Returns:
        dict with 'action' key: 'no_change', 'updated', or 'skipped'
    """
    registry_id = os.environ["REGISTRY_ID"]
    client = _get_registry_client()
    # Find the matching record
    record_id, full_record = _find_record_by_mcp_url(client, registry_id, mcp_url)
    if not record_id:
        print(f"No matching registry record found for {mcp_url}")
        return {"action": "skipped", "reason": "no matching record"}
    # Get current tools from registry
    registry_tools = _get_registry_tools_from_record(full_record)
    # Compare normalized tool lists
    mcp_normalized = _normalize_tools(mcp_tools)
    registry_normalized = _normalize_tools(registry_tools)
    if mcp_normalized == registry_normalized:
        print(f"No change detected. Registry record {record_id} is up to date ({len(registry_tools)} tools).")
        return {
            "action": "no_change",
            "record_id": record_id,
            "tool_count": len(registry_tools),
        }
    # Tools differ — update the registry
    print(f"Change detected! Registry has {len(registry_tools)} tools, MCP server has {len(mcp_tools)} tools.")
    # Log the diff
    mcp_names = {t["name"] for t in mcp_normalized}
    reg_names = {t["name"] for t in registry_normalized}
    added = mcp_names - reg_names
    removed = reg_names - mcp_names
    if added:
        print(f" Added: {added}")
    if removed:
        print(f" Removed: {removed}")
    if not added and not removed:
        print(" Tool definitions changed (same names, different schemas/descriptions)")
    tool_schema_content = json.dumps({"tools": mcp_tools})
    client.update_registry_record(
        registryId=registry_id,
        recordId=record_id,
        descriptors={
            "optionalValue": {
                "mcpServer": {
                    "optionalValue": {
                        "additionalData": {
                            "optionalValue": {
                                "tools": {
                                    "optionalValue": {
                                        "data": {
                                            "optionalValue": tool_schema_content,
                                        },
                                    }
                                }
                            }
                        }
                    }
                }
            }
        },
    )
    print(f"Updated registry record {record_id} in registry {registry_id}")
    return {
        "action": "updated",
        "record_id": record_id,
        "old_count": len(registry_tools),
        "new_count": len(mcp_tools),
    }&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="governance-and-approval-workflow"&gt;Governance and approval workflow&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AWS Agent Registry enforces a governance lifecycle on each record: &lt;strong&gt;Draft -&amp;gt; Pending Approval -&amp;gt; Approved&lt;/strong&gt; (or Rejected/Deprecated). Administrators configure approval settings at the registry level. These settings control what gets published, who reviews it, and whether records require manual approval before becoming discoverable. Only records in Approved status are visible to consumers through search, catalog browsing, or the registry’s MCP endpoint.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the Lambda updates a record with new tool definitions, the record automatically reverts to Draft status. Consumers continue to see the previously approved version. A curator or administrator must then approve the update before the new metadata becomes discoverable. This way the automated updates cannot bypass organizational review, and administrators retain full control over what is exposed to consumers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="deployment"&gt;Deployment&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table outlines steps in the automation pipeline:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Step&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Description&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Define configuration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Region, Lambda name, registry ID, and MCP server ARN&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create and approve registry record&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Register the MCP server entry and move it to APPROVED&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Provision AgentCore Identity&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create workload identity and OAuth2 credential providers&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Deploy Lambda function&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create IAM execution role, package handler, deploy function&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Create Amazon EventBridge rule&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Route &lt;code&gt;UpdateAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt; events to the Lambda&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cross-account forwarding (optional)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Forward runtime events from remote accounts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before deploying this solution, verify that the following are in place:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AWS account with permissions to create Lambda functions, Amazon EventBridge rules, AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) roles, and Agent Registry resources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;AWS Command Line Interface (AWS CLI) configured with credentials to access all the services used in the sample. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/cli/latest/userguide/getting-started-quickstart.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting started with the AWS CLI&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Python 3.12 runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An existing AWS Agent Registry with at least one MCP server record (created during initial onboarding; instructions to create the record are provided in the Deployment section).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An MCP server deployed on Amazon Bedrock AgentCore runtime.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;An AgentCore Identity workload identity and OAuth2 credential providers configured for each MCP server account (see &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/registry-push-sync-lambda" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt; for setup).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;For multi-account setups, cross-account Amazon EventBridge permissions between Account B and Account A.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Organizations often use multiple accounts, where the registry is in one account but the MCP servers are in others. This sample outlines the multi-account scenario, which you can convert to a single-account scenario by following the instructions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="creating-the-registry-record"&gt;Creating the registry record&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this sample, before the sync pipeline can work, a registry record must exist with the runtime ARN explicitly embedded in its server schema. You can use the following code to create the registry record and include the runtime ARN in the record’s &lt;code&gt;mcpServer.data&lt;/code&gt; field. This is what the Lambda matches against to correlate AWS CloudTrail events to records:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# The websiteUrl contains the MCP server invocation URL with the encoded
# runtime ARN: this is what the Lambda matches against later.
encoded_arn = MCP_RUNTIME_ARN.replace(":", "%3A").replace("/", "%2F")
mcp_server_url = (
    f"https://bedrock-agentcore.{AWS_REGION}.amazonaws.com"
    f"/runtimes/{encoded_arn}/invocations?qualifier=DEFAULT"
)
server_schema = json.dumps({
    "name": MCP_SERVER_NAME,
    "description": MCP_SERVER_DESCRIPTION,
    "websiteUrl": mcp_server_url,
})
registry_cp.create_registry_record(
    registryId=REGISTRY_ID,
    name=MCP_SERVER_NAME,
    description=MCP_SERVER_DESCRIPTION,
    recordType="MCP",
    recordVersion="1.0",
    descriptors={"mcpServer": {"data": server_schema}}
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="approving-the-record"&gt;Approving the record&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Lambda only syncs to APPROVED records.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;After making the update, the registry record is in the DRAFT stage. It must move through the governance workflow for the change to be discoverable.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Submit for approval (DRAFT → PENDING_APPROVAL)
registry_cp.submit_registry_record_for_approval(
    registryId=REGISTRY_ID,
    recordId=RECORD_ID,
)
# Approve (PENDING_APPROVAL → APPROVED)
registry_cp.update_registry_record_status(
    registryId=REGISTRY_ID,
    recordId=RECORD_ID,
    status="APPROVED",
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="lambda-environment-variables"&gt;Lambda environment variables&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To maintain a strong security posture, zero client secrets are stored in the Lambda environment variables. The function relies entirely on mapping environment variables to reference runtime workload identities, provider names, and permission scopes.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# No client secrets here. Only provider names and scopes.
# The Lambda uses WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_NAME to get a workload token,
# then CREDENTIAL_PROVIDER_{ACCT} to fetch the OAuth token.
env_vars = {
    "REGISTRY_ID": REGISTRY_ID,
    "WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_NAME": WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_NAME,
}
for acct_id, config in ACCOUNT_CONFIGS.items():
    env_vars[f"CREDENTIAL_PROVIDER_{acct_id}"] = config["provider_name"]
    env_vars[f"CREDENTIAL_SCOPE_{acct_id}"] = config.get("scope", "")&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="amazon-eventbridge-rule"&gt;Amazon EventBridge rule&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This rule acts as the pipeline trigger, listening for &lt;code&gt;UpdateAgentRuntime&lt;/code&gt; management events within the local account or arriving from cross-account Amazon EventBridge bus forwarders:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;# Match UpdateAgentRuntime events from AgentCore.
# Fires when any runtime is created or updated in this account
# (or forwarded from a cross-account EventBridge bus).
event_pattern = {
    "source": ["aws.bedrock-agentcore"],
    "detail-type": ["API Call via AWS CloudTrail"],
    "detail": {"eventName": ["UpdateAgentRuntime"]}
}
events_client.put_rule(
    Name=f"{LAMBDA_NAME}-trigger",
    EventPattern=json.dumps(event_pattern),
    State="ENABLED",
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="clean-up"&gt;Clean up&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To avoid ongoing charges, delete the following resources when you are done:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Lambda function&lt;/strong&gt; – Delete the push-sync Lambda and its IAM execution role.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Amazon EventBridge rule&lt;/strong&gt; – Delete the rule and its Lambda target.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;AgentCore Identity&lt;/strong&gt; – Delete the workload identity and OAuth2 credential providers.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Registry record&lt;/strong&gt; – Delete or deprecate the test MCP server record.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Registry&lt;/strong&gt; – Delete the registry if it was created solely for testing.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;(Multi-account)&lt;/strong&gt; Remove the cross-account Amazon EventBridge rule and resource policy in Account B.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can find the cleanup code in the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/registry-push-sync-lambda" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;sample repository&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="known-limitations-and-future-improvements"&gt;Known limitations and future improvements&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The sample code outlined here (and in the full sample) can be extended in several ways:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automatic registration of new MCP servers –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;Currently, a matching registry record must already exist. A future enhancement could automatically create a record when a previously unknown MCP server is detected for the first time.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Manual “force sync” trigger –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;For time-sensitive deployments, an on-demand invocation path through Amazon API Gateway or a CLI command can serve changes outside the regular CloudTrail delivery flow.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cold-start resilience –&lt;/strong&gt; If an MCP server is cold and returns zero tools, the Lambda currently doesn’t retry. Adding exponential backoff and skipping the update when the tool count is zero would improve reliability.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The AWS Agent Registry gives organizations a governed, discoverable catalog for their AI agents and MCP servers.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The push-sync Lambda pattern closes the freshness gap: when a server change is detected, the registry record is updated automatically and securely, within the constraints described in the Known limitations section. Updates revert the record to DRAFT status and must pass through the approval workflow before becoming discoverable again.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;As your agent fleet grows from a handful to hundreds of MCP servers, this pattern keeps your registry as the single source of truth, consistently fresh and reviewed.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="get-started"&gt;Get started&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The full implementation (Lambda code, deployment script, and architecture diagram) is available as a &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/registry-push-sync-lambda" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;ready-to-deploy sample&lt;/a&gt;. To deploy the solution in your environment, clone the &lt;a href="https://github.com/awslabs/agentcore-samples/tree/main/01-features/07-centralize-and-govern-your-ai-infrastructure/03-registry/03-advanced/registry-push-sync-lambda" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;repository&lt;/a&gt; and follow the deployment steps in the README.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;hr style="width: 100%"&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>How DXC Technology built a multi-model AI substrate on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/how-dxc-technology-built-a-multi-model-ai-substrate-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Asif Fouzi]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Wed, 11 Nov 2026 20:45:45 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Customer Solutions]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Intermediate (200)]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">0e166aea5fc5e99de63f67eb0887a9fb579fb51f</guid>

					<description>DXC Technology runs mission-critical managed IT services for regulated organizations across 70 countries. Learn how DXC built DCX OASIS, its Human+AI operating model, on a governed multi-model substrate in Amazon Bedrock, separating a resilient model substrate from a trusted harness that orchestrates, governs, and audits AI agents at scale.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Nick Tamburro of DXC Technology.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When an AI agent can act on a production server at a bank or a hospital, access to a capable foundation model is only the starting point. Recent public incidents, in which autonomous agents took destructive actions their operators never intended, show that the real risk is rarely the model alone. It’s an agent holding more authority than its task requires, with nothing in the architecture to stop it. Avoiding that starts with a substrate that governs model access at the infrastructure level.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; provides that substrate. Through a single managed interface, it gives DXC OASIS access to multiple foundation models: Anthropic Claude, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/ai/generative-ai/nova/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Nova&lt;/a&gt;, Meta Llama, and open-weight families. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; enforce content filtering, personally identifiable information (PII) redaction, and denied-topic policies uniformly across every model. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/privatelink/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS PrivateLink&lt;/a&gt; keeps inference traffic within the customer’s virtual private cloud (VPC), and serverless scaling avoids self-managed GPU infrastructure as a failure point. Because these are system-level controls rather than bolt-ons, they gave &lt;a href="https://dxc.com/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DXC Technology&lt;/a&gt; a governed foundation for what no model layer can deliver on its own: a trusted harness that orchestrates, governs, and audits AI agents across enterprise IT operations at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Model availability varies by AWS Region. See &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;DXC Technology runs managed IT services for some of the world’s most regulated organizations: banks, hospitals, and government agencies. With more than 115,000 employees across 70 countries, DXC operates mission-critical systems where resilience and governance are non-negotiable. When DXC set out to build DXC OASIS, the harness for mission-critical IT operations, the team separated the problem into two layers. The first is a governed model substrate built on Amazon Bedrock. The second is the trusted harness built on top: DXC’s own orchestration, governance, and assurance layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we explore how DXC built the resilient, flexible model substrate beneath DXC OASIS on Amazon Bedrock. We also show where DXC drew the line between what the substrate provides and what the harness must provide. Since general availability in March 2026, DXC OASIS has reduced resolution time by 95% on average across 56 measured use cases.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Across every model, customer data stays within the AWS data boundary: prompts and completions are not shared with third-party model providers and are not used to train the underlying foundation models.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;blockquote&gt;
 &lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;“Our customers operate complex, business-critical environments and need both choice and resilience in how they adopt AI. Amazon Bedrock provides access to a broad ecosystem of models with the governance, security, and operational controls required at enterprise scale. That flexibility allows us to use the right model for the right task, adapt as technology evolves, and stay focused on delivering measurable business outcomes for our customers.”&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
 &lt;p&gt;— Dan Gray, President, GIS, DXC Technology&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/blockquote&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;DXC OASIS separates the question of which model from the question of how work gets done. Amazon Bedrock is the substrate: a governed gateway to foundation models (FMs), reachable through one consistent API, with security and networking controls that keep traffic inside the AWS perimeter. The harness is everything DXC builds on top: orchestration across tools and systems, operational context drawn from 60 years of runbooks and escalation logic, policy enforcement, human-oversight gates, and full auditability of every agent action.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The design goals were:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Resilience: No single point of failure at the model layer.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Flexibility: Route each task to the most appropriate model on cost, latency, and capability.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Regulatory sovereignty: Meet the data-handling expectations of regulated tenants.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Safety by design: Bound what every agent can do, so that no action with operational impact runs without policy checks and, where required, human approval.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The main risk DXC designed against was coupling the system to any single provider, framework, or deployment target. The following diagram shows the key AWS services in the DXC DXC OASIS architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Engineers and operators reach the system through Amazon Route 53 and an Application Load Balancer inside the customer’s &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/vpc/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC)&lt;/a&gt;. The DXC OASIS agent runtime runs on &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/eks/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Elastic Kubernetes Service (Amazon EKS)&lt;/a&gt; in a private subnet. There, a supervisor agent delegates to capability agents that draw on a knowledge base, AWS Secrets Manager, Amazon EventBridge, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/s3/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3)&lt;/a&gt;. Every model call leaves the subnet through a VPC interface endpoint and AWS PrivateLink to reach Amazon Bedrock, so inference traffic doesn’t traverse the public internet. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management (AWS IAM)&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/kms/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS)&lt;/a&gt;, TLS 1.2, Amazon CloudWatch, VPC Flow Logs, AWS CloudTrail, and OpenTelemetry provide security and observability across every layer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;a href="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ml-21702-arch.png" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;&lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/11/10/ml-21702-arch.png" alt="DXC OASIS architecture: an Amazon EKS agent runtime in a private VPC reaching Amazon Bedrock through AWS PrivateLink" width="800"&gt;&lt;/a&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;Figure 1: The DXC OASIS architecture on AWS&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="building-the-oasis-substrate"&gt;Building the DXC OASIS substrate&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;DXC began building DXC OASIS as a prototype in early 2025. A cross-functional team reached general availability in March 2026, scaling toward 130 customer environments on Amazon EKS with per-tenant namespaces and GitOps rollout. Building the substrate involved the following tasks:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Establish governed, multi-model access through Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Engineer resilience into every layer of the architecture.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Route each workload to the most appropriate model.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Draw the line between the substrate and the harness.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="establish-governed-multi-model-access-through-amazon-bedrock"&gt;Establish governed, multi-model access through Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every model DXC OASIS uses (Anthropic Claude, Amazon Nova, Meta Llama, and open-weight families) is reached through Amazon Bedrock model inference. A provider outage or deprecation therefore becomes a routing decision, not a system rebuild. Uniform access isn’t the same as uniform behavior, however: models differ in capability, response style, and failure modes. Substituting one model for another is an assurance activity as much as a routing change. Before a model enters production routing, the harness (not the API) evaluates it against DXC OASIS task suites. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/getting-started.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Getting started with Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="engineer-resilience-into-every-layer"&gt;Engineer resilience into every layer&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Resilience in DXC OASIS isn’t one control but the compounding of several. The following table summarizes the resilience controls and what each does for DXC OASIS.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resilience control&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What it does for DXC OASIS&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;No single-provider dependency&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Every model is reached through Amazon Bedrock model inference. A provider outage or deprecation is a routing decision rather than a system rebuild, with the harness assuring behavioral differences between models before a switch goes live.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Serverless model layer&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock is serverless and the Amazon EKS control plane is AWS managed, removing single points of failure.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Scope-of-impact containment&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails apply content filters, denied topics, PII filters, and contextual controls consistently regardless of model, containing the scope of any single failure.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Private, encrypted paths&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon VPC and AWS PrivateLink keep model traffic off the public internet. TLS 1.2 in transit and AWS KMS at rest protect data.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Elastic capacity&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon EKS autoscaling with per-tenant namespaces makes onboarding a new tenant a capacity-planning exercise.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Beyond resilience, DXC OASIS also addresses data residency and sovereignty. Regional inference controls and AWS PrivateLink keep inference within approved AWS Regions and off the public internet, meeting the data-handling expectations of regulated tenants.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="route-each-workload-to-the-most-appropriate-model"&gt;Route each workload to the most appropriate model&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The strategic reason DXC built on Amazon Bedrock is optionality. Because every model sits behind the same API, the team routes each task to the most appropriate model on cost, latency, and capability, and adopts new frontier models as they arrive without re-plumbing the system.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="model-choice-as-a-workload-decision"&gt;Model choice as a workload decision&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Each task’s capability, latency, and cost requirements determine which model it uses, not system constraints.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Intelligent prompt routing –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A single serverless endpoint predicts response quality and routes requests between models in the same family. It sends simpler prompts to lower-cost models and complex reasoning to more capable ones. Prompt caching further cuts latency and cost. For more information, see &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-routing.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Intelligent prompt routing&lt;/a&gt; in the Amazon Bedrock documentation.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Custom model import –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;With &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-customization-import-model.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Custom Model Import&lt;/a&gt;, the same governance and tooling extend to models that DXC or its clients bring themselves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h4 id="open-and-closed-models-together"&gt;Open and closed models together&lt;/h4&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock supports open-weight models alongside proprietary ones, so DXC pairs frontier reasoning with open-weight models a client can adapt and deploy under one set of guardrails.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="draw-the-line-between-the-substrate-and-the-harness"&gt;Draw the line between the substrate and the harness&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every enterprise agent needs six capabilities. Some are substrate capabilities Amazon Bedrock provides directly. Others are things only the harness can provide because they depend on DXC’s operational context and accountability model. This boundary is also where agent safety is won or lost. Guardrails on model inputs and outputs are necessary, but only the harness can limit what an agent is allowed to do. The following table draws that line.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Capability&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What Bedrock provides&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What the harness adds&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Orchestration&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;A foundational service layer the harness calls, wraps, or extends.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Supervisor and capability-agent delegation across tools, systems, and multi-step workflows, built on the open source LangGraph runtime.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Context&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Knowledge-base infrastructure and prompt caching for retrieval.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;60 years of runbooks, escalation logic, and live IT service management (ITSM) data.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Governance&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;AWS IAM, regional inference controls, and tenant-isolated model access.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Per-request policy enforcement, persona-based authorization, and field-level constraints.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Guardrails&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails: content filters, denied topics, PII filters.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Operational-policy enforcement above the substrate filters: tiered knowledge hierarchy, scoped and auditable agent roles, and customer-specific rules on what an agent can act on.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Human oversight&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Not a substrate function.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Four authority tiers (observe, propose, act with approval, act within scope), with every persona starting read-only, plus approval gates and escalation paths.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Assurance&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Encryption, auditable infrastructure, and VPC Flow Logs.&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;OpenTelemetry traces for every action, each with a unique execution ID, giving end-to-end traceability from intent to action.&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-a-workload-flows-through-oasis"&gt;How a workload flows through DXC OASIS&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To make the architecture concrete, the following use cases come from the DXC OASIS demo environment. The environment is built around AnyCompany Airlines, a fictional mid-sized airline running a hybrid on-premises and AWS estate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Incident resolution:&lt;/strong&gt; Diagnose and fix an at-risk production server, from alert to resolution in under 10 minutes.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Problem management:&lt;/strong&gt; The ITSM agent ranks the top five incident categories and suggests how to reduce each, work that takes 2–3 hours by hand.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cloud cost analysis:&lt;/strong&gt; For the AWS cloud engineer, the Metrics agent breaks down a 90-day cost trend by service and region, then investigates spending peaks on request.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The incident-resolution workflow shows how the substrate and the harness work together:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Alert in business terms –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;A runbook fails to clear disk space on the FlightOps server. The CIO sees the alert as a business risk: Flight Operations scheduling might be affected within 4 hours. At the same moment, the Linux engineer sees the same alert with the technical detail needed to act.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Plan and delegate –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The engineer opens AI Assist from the dashboard widget, and the metric carries into the chat as context. A supervisor agent breaks the request into a plan and delegates to capability agents (DXC OASIS Metrics, Linux System, and ITSM), with every model call made through Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Grounded diagnosis –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The DXC OASIS Metrics agent confirms the server is 99% full and cites the knowledge base articles behind its answer. The Linux System agent traces the problem to a single 5 GB temporary file.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Human approval –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The agent presents its full execution plan, and nothing runs until the engineer confirms it. In this workflow, the agent operates at the act-with-approval tier: it can diagnose freely, but it can’t change the production server without an explicit, recorded approval.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Governed execution –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The fix runs, and DXC OASIS records what ran, on which server, who approved it, and when. The audit trail comes from the harness, not from the agent’s own account of what it did.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Learn and observe –&lt;/strong&gt;&amp;nbsp;The engineer can turn the conversation into a draft knowledge article. The Query Metrics view shows the agent timeline, delegation flow, and token usage behind the answer.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Since reaching general availability in March 2026, DXC OASIS agents now serve 12 operational personas, spanning Linux, Windows, SAP, virtualization, backup and patching, AWS, Azure, and network operations. Across 56 measured use cases, DXC OASIS has reduced resolution time by 95 percent on average. The following table shows representative measurements.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Task&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Before&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;After&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Time saved&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Filesystem incident&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;53–90 mins&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;3–6 mins&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~89–97%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Database performance incident&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2–3 hours&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5–12 mins&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~90–97%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Cloud policy validation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;4–8 hours&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;15–30 mins&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~88–97%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Patching validation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;45–90 mins&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;5–10 mins&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~85–90%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Complex problem management&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;2–6 days&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;8–10 mins&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;~99%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Diagnostic accuracy is 92% or better, validated against senior engineer judgment.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="lessons-learned-and-what-comes-next"&gt;Lessons learned and what comes next&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Four architectural decisions proved most valuable:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="separate-model-choice-from-orchestration"&gt;Separate model choice from orchestration&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;By keeping model selection, orchestration framework, and deployment target as independent decisions, DXC avoids re-platforming when a layer evolves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="compound-resilience-controls"&gt;Compound resilience controls&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;No single control makes the system resilient. The combination of serverless infrastructure, guardrails at the model layer, private networking, and human-oversight gates creates defense in depth.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="draw-the-substrate-harness-line-explicitly"&gt;Draw the substrate-harness line explicitly&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Clarity about what the cloud provides versus what you must build avoids both under-engineering (trusting the model to govern itself) and over-engineering (rebuilding capabilities the substrate already offers).&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Bound autonomy before you scale it. Every DXC OASIS persona starts read-only, and broader authority is granted deliberately, one tier at a time. Treating autonomy as something granted per task, rather than a default, is what lets DXC put agents in front of production systems with confidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="compound-resilience-controls"&gt;Bound autonomy before you scale it&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Every DXC OASIS persona starts read-only and broader authority is granted deliberately, one tier at a time. Treating autonomy as something granted per task, rather than a default, is what lets DXC put agents in front of production systems with confidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Looking ahead, DXC is evaluating &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/agentcore/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock AgentCore&lt;/a&gt; alongside the current orchestration layer, and expanding the system to support additional regulated workloads as new frontier models become available through Amazon Bedrock.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we showed how DXC Technology built DXC OASIS, the harness for mission-critical IT operations, on a governed, multi-model substrate in Amazon Bedrock. Keeping model reasoning separate from operational orchestration builds resilience, flexibility, and regulatory compliance into the architecture from the start. The payoff is measurable: resolution time is down 95 percent on average across 56 measured use cases. For teams building their own agentic systems, the lesson is to treat the substrate (what Amazon Bedrock provides) and the harness (what you build on top) as separate layers. Decide early which capabilities belong in each. As scrutiny of autonomous AI grows, that clear line, together with bounded, auditable agent authority, is what lets a system earn trust at scale.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started with multi-model architectures on Amazon Bedrock, see the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock User Guide&lt;/a&gt;. To learn more about how &lt;a href="https://dxc.com/platforms/dxc-oasis" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;DXC OASIS&lt;/a&gt; applies this architecture to enterprise IT operations, contact DXC Technology.&lt;/p&gt;

	<item>
		<title>Building the next generation of multimodal AI experiences with fal in Microsoft Foundry</title>
		<link>https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azure-ai-foundry-blog/building-the-next-generation-of-multimodal-ai-experiences-with-fal-in-microsoft-/4570032</link>
					<comments>https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azure-ai-foundry-blog/building-the-next-generation-of-multimodal-ai-experiences-with-fal-in-microsoft-/4570032#respond</comments>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Naomi Moneypenny]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 06 Nov 2026 19:08:44 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[AI + machine learning]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog/?p=54939</guid>

					<description><![CDATA[<p>Microsoft and fal partner to expand video generation in Microsoft Foundry, helping developers bring creative ideas into enterprise applications.</p>
<p>The post <a href="https://techcommunity.microsoft.com/blog/azure-ai-foundry-blog/building-the-next-generation-of-multimodal-ai-experiences-with-fal-in-microsoft-/4570032">Building the next generation of multimodal AI experiences with fal in Microsoft Foundry</a> appeared first on <a href="https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/blog">Microsoft Azure Blog</a>.</p>
]]></description>
										<content:encoded><![CDATA[
<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Microsoft and fal partner to expand video generation in Microsoft Foundry, helping developers bring creative ideas into enterprise applications.<br>Video workloads do not all start with the same input or aim for the same result. A creative team might explore several interpretations of a written brief. A retailer might turn an approved product image into a short motion concept. An application developer might combine image generation and video generation into a workflow that takes an idea from description to frame to scene. Each scenario brings different requirements for visual quality, motion, creative control, turnaround time, and cost. All of this means that a multi-model solution is necessary to realize a full vision.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Today, Microsoft and fal are helping developers find the right tools for their workloads. We are excited to announce that Kling, LTX, and MiniMax video models are rolling out to in public preview in Microsoft Foundry through our new partnership with fal. Available as a first-party Azure service, these text-to-video and image-to-video options give creators and developers more ways to utilize video generation with the experiences they want to build.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The partnership expands the choices available in Microsoft Foundry while bringing video into the broader platform developers use to build AI applications. The goal is to let developers pick the right model for each step of an experience, then connect those steps into a working application.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">“fal has seen incredible adoption this year alone with generative media across all industries from advertising to marketing to gaming. Partnering with Microsoft allows both of us to truly enable the wide creative ecosystem and ensure that all AI solutions that lean on generative media, becomes a multi-model one.” &#8211; Burkay Gur, Cofounder</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Bringing fal’s generative media focus to enterprise builders<br>fal is the full stack for generative media, built for creators and developers, with APIs for over 1500 models across image, video, audio, 3D, and generative worlds. As a truly encapsulating platform, fal provides inference, training, and infrastructure to run generative media workloads in production, while also providing innovative post trained models that pushes the boundaries on speed and quality.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">That specialization matters because generative media is both a creative and an engineering workload. A compelling demo is only the start. Production applications also need to handle real traffic, scale with demand, and balance output quality, latency, and cost.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Through this partnership, developers will be able to access a selected set of video models in Microsoft Foundry, served by fal and sourced from the companies that build them. The initial portfolio gives teams a choice of models for different creative requirements instead of relying on one model for every task, with more models planned over time.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">A portfolio for different video experiences<br>The initial portfolio spans Kling v3 Pro, LTX-2.5 Fast, LTX-2.5 Pro, MiniMax H3 Max, and MiniMax H3 Max Turbo. These models give developers different starting points for generating video from text and animating existing images. A team rapidly exploring creative concepts may prioritize speed and cost, while a team producing fewer, higher-fidelity assets may weigh output quality more heavily. Whether an application starts from text or an existing image, and what it needs in output quality and cost, also shapes the choice.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Kling: From a still image to a directed scene</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Kling v3 Pro combines cinematic visuals and fluid motion with native audio generation and custom element support. Its image-to-video capabilities give developers control over how a starting image becomes a moving scene, a fit for applications that prioritize visual direction and motion.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">LTX: One model for fast drafts, one for final output</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">For rapid iteration and previews, LTX-2.5 Fast is the speed-optimized option; for high-fidelity final output, LTX-2.5 Pro is the quality-optimized option. Both support text-to-video and image-to-video generation with synchronized audio.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">MiniMax: Another approach to video generation</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">MiniMax H3 Max and MiniMax H3 Max Turbo are post-trained variants of MiniMax H3 focused on prompt adherence, aesthetics, and inference performance. For developers, they provide additional options to evaluate against an application’s creative brief, quality requirements, and performance needs.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">“When building with video models, the details matter: how closely the output follows your prompt, how well it preserves your reference images/videos/audios and how quickly you can try another idea. Kling gives developers control over detailed reference to video generation. LTX offers fast iteration and high-quality output through its Fast and Pro variants. H3 Max brings fal&#8217;s post-training and inference optimizations to improve quality and speed. Having these models available through Microsoft Foundry gives developers more flexibility to choose what works best for the application they are building.&#8221; &#8211; Gökay Aydoğan, Creative Engineer</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Built for enterprise video workloads<br>Bringing video generation into an enterprise application requires more than choosing the right model. Teams need a platform where they can evaluate different options, build with the models that fit their requirements, and operate those experiences alongside the rest of their applications.</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">The initial portfolio of video models is hosted in the US, and Microsoft Foundry is the first provider to offer a US-hosted Kling endpoint. Key considerations for enterprise teams:</p>



<p class="wp-block-paragraph">Data residency: For organizations with data residency requirements, hosting location is one factor to weigh. Review each model&#8217;s documented data processing and storage terms to confirm it meets your requirements.<br>Model evaluation: Teams can assess quality, performance, and cost, then connect video generation with the other models and capabilities their applications need.<br>Production readiness: As they move toward production, they can use the evaluation, optimization, governance, security, observability, and infrastructure capabilities supported for their selected offering.<br>Pricing<br>Video generation pricing varies by model and output resolution, giving teams options to balance creative requirements with their application’s budget. The table below shows pricing in USD per minute of generated video for LTX and MiniMax models, making it easier to compare costs across the available options.</p>
